<!-- source: notes/a-level/further-maths/01-proof-by-induction.md -->

## Proof by induction

This file covers proof by mathematical induction: sums of series, divisibility, powers of matrices, terms of sequences, and harder results such as inequalities and derivatives. It also sets out the overarching themes (argument, problem solving and modelling) that AQA lists as OT1 to OT3.

### Overview

Induction proves that a statement P(n) is true for every integer n from some starting value. It builds on proof by deduction, exhaustion, counter-example and contradiction from A-level Maths ([../maths/01-proof-and-mathematical-argument.md](../maths/01-proof-and-mathematical-argument.md)).

#### Board differences

- AQA A1 and Edexcel CP1.1 name the contexts: sums of series, divisibility and powers of matrices. Both are one statement for AS and A-level.
- OCR 4.01a (Stage 1) is induction for given results; 4.01b (Stage 2) adds "more demanding" proofs, including making a conjecture first and then proving it.
- MEI Pp4 (AS) limits induction to the nth term of a sequence, the sum of a series and the nth power of a matrix. Pp5 (A-level) opens it to any context, such as divisibility and de Moivre's theorem. MEI says the result to be proved will always be given.
- Recurrence relation proofs (Edexcel FP2 6.3, OCR 8.01b) are in [26-sequences-and-recurrence-relations.md](26-sequences-and-recurrence-relations.md). De Moivre's theorem is proved in [04-complex-numbers-de-moivre-exponential-form-and-nth-roots.md](04-complex-numbers-de-moivre-exponential-form-and-nth-roots.md).

### OT1 to OT3 Argument, problem solving and modelling

*Refs: AQA OT1.1 to OT3.5. Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- Present a chain of reasoning with correct notation (⇒, ⇔, ∴, ∈, set notation) and say clearly what has been shown.
- Critique a given argument: find the step that fails, or a missing case.
- Solve problems that are not broken into parts: decide which technique to use, and check the answer makes sense.
- Model a situation: state assumptions, use the model, interpret the output in context, and refine the model when the output disagrees with reality.

#### Explained

These themes run through every Further Maths topic rather than being a topic of their own. In practice they mean three habits.

1. **Say what you are proving and how.** Start a proof with the statement, end with a conclusion that matches it.
2. **Check the structure.** Before calculating, ask what kind of problem it is: a polynomial whose roots you need (file 07), a system of equations (file 06), a rate of change (files 16 and 17).
3. **Interpret.** A modelling answer is not finished until it is a sentence about the situation, with units and a comment on whether it is sensible.

#### Worked example

Critique this "proof" that every 2 × 2 matrix A with A² = I is I or −I: "A² − I = 0, so (A − I)(A + I) = 0, so A − I = 0 or A + I = 0."

1. For matrices, a product can be the zero matrix when neither factor is zero.
2. Counter-example: A = [[1, 0], [0, −1]] gives A² = I but A is neither I nor −I.
3. The faulty step is "XY = 0 implies X = 0 or Y = 0", which holds for numbers but not matrices.

#### Common mistakes

- Treating matrices like numbers (cancelling or dividing).
- Ending a modelling question with a number and no comment on its meaning.

### A1 The method of induction and sums of series

*Refs: AQA A1 · Edexcel CP1.1 · OCR 4.01a, 4.01b · MEI Pp4, Pp5. Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- The four parts of an induction proof: basis, assumption, inductive step, conclusion.
- How to prove a formula for a finite sum, such as Σr³ = ¼n²(n + 1)².

#### Explained

Think of a row of dominoes. If the first falls, and each falling domino knocks over the next, all of them fall.

1. **Basis.** Show P(1) is true (or P(n₀) for the first value in the question).
2. **Assumption.** Assume P(k) is true for some integer k ≥ 1.
3. **Inductive step.** Use the assumption to show P(k + 1) is true.
4. **Conclusion.** Write it in full, for example: "P(1) is true, and if P(k) is true then P(k + 1) is true. Hence, by mathematical induction, P(n) is true for all positive integers n."

For sums, the key move is

```latex
\sum_{r=1}^{k+1} f(r) = \sum_{r=1}^{k} f(r) + f(k+1)
```

then replace the first sum with the assumed formula and simplify towards the formula with n = k + 1. Write down the target first, so you know what you are aiming at. Take out common factors early rather than expanding everything.

#### Worked example

Prove that Σ_{r=1}^{n} r(r + 2) = n(n + 1)(2n + 7)/6 for all positive integers n.

1. Basis, n = 1: left side 1 × 3 = 3. Right side 1 × 2 × 9/6 = 3. True.
2. Assume Σ_{r=1}^{k} r(r + 2) = k(k + 1)(2k + 7)/6.
3. Target: (k + 1)(k + 2)(2k + 9)/6.
4. Σ_{r=1}^{k+1} r(r + 2) = k(k + 1)(2k + 7)/6 + (k + 1)(k + 3)
   = (k + 1)[k(2k + 7) + 6(k + 3)]/6
   = (k + 1)(2k² + 13k + 18)/6
   = (k + 1)(k + 2)(2k + 9)/6.
5. This is the formula with n = k + 1. Conclusion as above.

Prove that Σ_{r=1}^{n} 1/[r(r + 1)] = n/(n + 1).

1. n = 1: 1/2 on both sides.
2. Assume true for n = k. Then the sum to k + 1 is k/(k + 1) + 1/[(k + 1)(k + 2)].
3. = [k(k + 2) + 1]/[(k + 1)(k + 2)] = (k + 1)²/[(k + 1)(k + 2)] = (k + 1)/(k + 2).
4. True for n = k + 1, so true for all n ≥ 1.

Prove that Σ_{r=1}^{n} r × 2^r = (n − 1)2^{n+1} + 2.

1. n = 1: 2 = 0 + 2. True.
2. Assume true for n = k. Sum to k + 1 = (k − 1)2^{k+1} + 2 + (k + 1)2^{k+1} = 2k × 2^{k+1} + 2 = k × 2^{k+2} + 2.
3. Target ((k + 1) − 1)2^{k+2} + 2 = k × 2^{k+2} + 2. Matches, so true for all n ≥ 1.

#### Common mistakes

- Writing "assume true for n = k" and then using it as if it were proved for all k. The assumption is only a hypothesis.
- Checking n = 1 and n = 2 numerically and calling that a proof.
- A weak conclusion such as "so it works". The conclusion must state the logic.

### A1 Divisibility

*Refs: AQA A1 · Edexcel CP1.1 · OCR 4.01b · MEI Pp5. Level: A-level only on OCR and MEI; AQA and Edexcel do not separate.*

#### What you need to know

- Prove statements such as "7ⁿ − 1 is divisible by 6 for all n ≥ 1".

#### Explained

Let f(n) be the expression. Two standard approaches:

- **Difference method.** Show f(k + 1) − f(k) (or f(k + 1) − m f(k) for a well-chosen m) is a multiple of the divisor. Then f(k + 1) = f(k) + (multiple), and both terms on the right are multiples.
- **Substitution method.** From the assumption write f(k) = d × M for an integer M, rearrange to get one power in terms of M, and substitute into f(k + 1).

#### Worked example

Prove that 7ⁿ − 1 is divisible by 6 for all positive integers n.

1. n = 1: 6, divisible by 6.
2. Assume 7ᵏ − 1 = 6M for some integer M.
3. 7^{k+1} − 1 = 7 × 7ᵏ − 1 = 7(6M + 1) − 1 = 42M + 6 = 6(7M + 1).
4. Divisible by 6, so true for all n ≥ 1.

Prove that 3^{2n} + 11 is divisible by 4 for all n ≥ 1.

1. f(1) = 9 + 11 = 20 = 4 × 5.
2. f(k + 1) − f(k) = 9 × 3^{2k} − 3^{2k} = 8 × 3^{2k}, a multiple of 4.
3. So f(k + 1) = f(k) + 8 × 3^{2k}. If f(k) is a multiple of 4, so is f(k + 1).
4. True for n = 1 and true for k + 1 whenever true for k, so true for all n ≥ 1.

Prove that n³ + 5n is divisible by 6 for all n ≥ 1.

1. f(1) = 6.
2. f(k + 1) = (k + 1)³ + 5(k + 1) = k³ + 3k² + 3k + 1 + 5k + 5 = (k³ + 5k) + 3k(k + 1) + 6.
3. k³ + 5k is a multiple of 6 by assumption. k(k + 1) is a product of consecutive integers, so it is even, and 3k(k + 1) is a multiple of 6. 6 is a multiple of 6.
4. So f(k + 1) is divisible by 6. By induction, true for all n ≥ 1.

#### Common mistakes

- Forgetting to say why each part of the sum is divisible.
- Writing f(k) = 6M without saying M is an integer.

### A1 Powers of matrices

*Refs: AQA A1 · Edexcel CP1.1 · OCR 4.01a · MEI Pp4. Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- Prove a formula for Mⁿ, using M^{k+1} = MᵏM (or MMᵏ).

#### Explained

Assume the formula for Mᵏ, multiply by M once, and simplify each entry. Matrix multiplication is not commutative in general, but M commutes with its own powers, so either order works. Matrix multiplication is in [05-matrices-operations-and-transformations.md](05-matrices-operations-and-transformations.md).

#### Worked example

Prove that for M = [[3, −2], [1, 0]], Mⁿ = [[2^{n+1} − 1, 2 − 2^{n+1}], [2ⁿ − 1, 2 − 2ⁿ]] for all n ≥ 1.

1. n = 1: [[4 − 1, 2 − 4], [2 − 1, 2 − 2]] = [[3, −2], [1, 0]] = M. True.
2. Assume Mᵏ = [[2^{k+1} − 1, 2 − 2^{k+1}], [2ᵏ − 1, 2 − 2ᵏ]].
3. M^{k+1} = MᵏM. Top left: 3(2^{k+1} − 1) + (2 − 2^{k+1}) = 2 × 2^{k+1} − 1 = 2^{k+2} − 1.
   Top right: −2(2^{k+1} − 1) + 0 = 2 − 2^{k+2}.
   Bottom left: 3(2ᵏ − 1) + 2 − 2ᵏ = 2^{k+1} − 1.
   Bottom right: −2(2ᵏ − 1) = 2 − 2^{k+1}.
4. This is the formula with n = k + 1, so true for all n ≥ 1.

Prove that [[1, a], [0, 1]]ⁿ = [[1, na], [0, 1]].

1. n = 1: true.
2. Assume true for k. [[1, ka], [0, 1]][[1, a], [0, 1]] = [[1, a + ka], [0, 1]] = [[1, (k + 1)a], [0, 1]].
3. True for all n ≥ 1. (Geometrically: n shears of size a make a shear of size na.)

#### Common mistakes

- Multiplying in a way that does not match your assumption, then mixing the order.
- Only checking one entry.

### A1 Sequences, inequalities and derivatives

*Refs: AQA A1 · Edexcel CP1.1 · OCR 4.01b · MEI Pp4, Pp5. Level: nth term of a sequence AS; the rest A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- Prove a formula for the nth term of a sequence defined by a recurrence.
- (A-level only) Prove inequalities and results about nth derivatives, and conjecture a formula from the first few cases before proving it (OCR 4.01b).

#### Explained

For a sequence u_{n+1} = g(uₙ), assume uₖ equals the formula and substitute into the recurrence to get u_{k+1}.

For an inequality, the inductive step usually needs one extra fact. To show f(k + 1) > g(k + 1), use the assumption f(k) > g(k), then show the remaining gap is positive.

For derivatives, differentiate the assumed formula for the kth derivative once to get the (k + 1)th.

#### Worked example

A sequence has u₁ = 2 and u_{n+1} = 3uₙ − 2. Prove that uₙ = 3^{n−1} + 1.

1. n = 1: 3⁰ + 1 = 2. True.
2. Assume uₖ = 3^{k−1} + 1. Then u_{k+1} = 3(3^{k−1} + 1) − 2 = 3ᵏ + 1, the formula for n = k + 1.
3. True for all n ≥ 1.

(A-level only) Prove that 2ⁿ > n² for all integers n ≥ 5.

1. n = 5: 32 > 25. True.
2. Assume 2ᵏ > k² for some k ≥ 5. Then 2^{k+1} = 2 × 2ᵏ > 2k².
3. Need 2k² ≥ (k + 1)², that is k² − 2k − 1 ≥ 0, which holds for k ≥ 3 (k² − 2k − 1 = (k − 1)² − 2 ≥ 2 when k ≥ 3).
4. So 2^{k+1} > (k + 1)². True for all n ≥ 5.

(A-level only) Let y = xeˣ. Find the first three derivatives, conjecture dⁿy/dxⁿ, and prove it.

1. y′ = (x + 1)eˣ, y″ = (x + 2)eˣ, y‴ = (x + 3)eˣ. Conjecture: dⁿy/dxⁿ = (x + n)eˣ.
2. n = 1: true.
3. Assume dᵏy/dxᵏ = (x + k)eˣ. Differentiating: eˣ + (x + k)eˣ = (x + k + 1)eˣ.
4. True for all n ≥ 1.

#### Common mistakes

- Starting at n = 1 when the statement only holds from n = 5.
- In inequalities, writing a chain like 2^{k+1} > 2k² > (k + 1)² without justifying the second step.

### Check questions

1. Prove by induction that Σ_{r=1}^{n} (2r − 1)² = n(2n − 1)(2n + 1)/3 for all positive integers n. [5 marks]
2. Prove by induction that 5ⁿ + 3 is divisible by 4 for all positive integers n. [4 marks]
3. M = [[2, 1], [0, 1]]. Prove by induction that Mⁿ = [[2ⁿ, 2ⁿ − 1], [0, 1]] for all positive integers n. [5 marks]
4. A sequence is defined by u₁ = 1, u_{n+1} = 2uₙ + 3. Prove that uₙ = 2^{n+1} − 3. [4 marks]
5. Prove by induction that 4ⁿ + 6n − 1 is divisible by 9 for all positive integers n. [5 marks]
6. (A-level only) Prove that 3ⁿ > 2n + 1 for all integers n ≥ 2. [4 marks]
7. (A-level only) f(x) = e^{2x}. Prove by induction that the nth derivative of x e^{2x} is 2^{n−1}(2x + n)e^{2x}. [5 marks]
8. A student claims: "Since n² + n + 41 is prime for n = 1, 2, ..., 10, it is prime for every positive integer n." Explain why this is not a proof, and show the claim is false. [3 marks]

### Mark scheme

1. Basis: 1 = 1 × 1 × 3/3 (1). Assume for k; add (2k + 1)² (1). Take out (2k + 1)/3: (2k + 1)[k(2k − 1) + 3(2k + 1)]/3 (1) = (2k + 1)(2k² + 5k + 3)/3 = (k + 1)(2k + 1)(2k + 3)/3 (1). Correct conclusion (1).
2. n = 1: 8 (1). Assume 5ᵏ + 3 = 4M (1). 5^{k+1} + 3 = 5(4M − 3) + 3 = 20M − 12 = 4(5M − 3) (1). Conclusion (1).
3. n = 1 gives M (1). Assume for k (1). MᵏM = [[2^{k+1}, 2ᵏ + 2ᵏ − 1], [0, 1]] (2) = [[2^{k+1}, 2^{k+1} − 1], [0, 1]] and conclusion (1).
4. u₁ = 4 − 3 = 1 (1). Assume uₖ = 2^{k+1} − 3 (1). u_{k+1} = 2^{k+2} − 6 + 3 = 2^{k+2} − 3 (1). Conclusion (1).
5. f(1) = 9 (1). f(k + 1) − 4f(k) = 4^{k+1} + 6k + 5 − 4^{k+1} − 24k + 4 = 9 − 18k (2). So f(k + 1) = 4f(k) + 9(1 − 2k), a multiple of 9 (1). Conclusion (1).
6. n = 2: 9 > 5 (1). Assume 3ᵏ > 2k + 1; 3^{k+1} > 6k + 3 (1). 6k + 3 − (2k + 3) = 4k > 0, so 3^{k+1} > 2(k + 1) + 1 (1). Conclusion (1).
7. n = 1: (1 + 2x)e^{2x}, matches 2⁰(2x + 1)e^{2x} (1). Assume kth derivative 2^{k−1}(2x + k)e^{2x} (1). Differentiate: 2^{k−1}[2e^{2x} + 2(2x + k)e^{2x}] (1) = 2ᵏ(2x + k + 1)e^{2x} (1). Conclusion (1).
8. Checking cases does not show it holds for all n; there is no inductive step (1). n = 40: 1600 + 40 + 41 = 1681 = 41² (1), which is not prime (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/further-maths/22-numerical-integration-and-step-by-step-methods.md -->

## Numerical integration and step-by-step methods for differential equations

This file covers the mid-ordinate (midpoint) rule and Simpson's rule, how the midpoint, trapezium and Simpson's rules relate, Euler's method, improved and modified Euler methods, the Runge-Kutta idea, and step-by-step methods for second order equations.

### Overview

The trapezium rule is in A-level Maths ([../maths/17-numerical-methods.md](../maths/17-numerical-methods.md)). Solving equations numerically, errors and interpolation (MEI Numerical Methods) are in [60-numerical-methods-errors-equations-and-approximation.md](60-numerical-methods-errors-equations-and-approximation.md). Analytic solutions of differential equations are in [16-differential-equations-first-order.md](16-differential-equations-first-order.md) and [17-differential-equations-second-order-and-oscillations.md](17-differential-equations-second-order-and-oscillations.md).

#### Board differences

- AQA J1 (mid-ordinate and Simpson's rules), J2 (Euler), J3 (improved Euler, in the form y_{r+1} = y_{r−1} + 2hf(x_r, y_r)).
- Edexcel FP1 6.1 uses the approximations (dy/dx)ₙ ≈ (y_{n+1} − yₙ)/h and (dy/dx)ₙ ≈ (y_{n+1} − y_{n−1})/(2h), and (d²y/dx²)ₙ ≈ (y_{n+1} − 2yₙ + y_{n−1})/h² for second order equations. FP1 6.2 is Simpson's rule.
- MEI Nc3, Nc4: midpoint, trapezium and Simpson's rules, T₂ₙ = ½(Mₙ + Tₙ), S₂ₙ = (2Mₙ + Tₙ)/3, and the order of each method. MEI Tc7 to Tc10: Euler, a modified Euler method (k₁ = hf(xₙ, yₙ), k₂ = hf(xₙ + h, yₙ + k₁), y_{n+1} = yₙ + ½(k₁ + k₂)) and fourth order Runge-Kutta, with software.
- Note the name clash: AQA's "improved Euler" is the two-step formula above; MEI's "modified Euler" is the predictor-corrector one. Use the version your board gives.

### J1 The mid-ordinate rule and Simpson's rule

*Refs: AQA J1 · Edexcel FP1 6.2 · MEI Nc3, Nc4. Level: A-level only (MEI also AS).*

#### What you need to know

With n strips of width h = (b − a)/n:

```latex
M_n = h\,(y_{1/2} + y_{3/2} + \dots + y_{n-1/2})
```

With an even number 2n of strips of width h = (b − a)/(2n):

```latex
S_{2n} = \frac{h}{3}\big[(y_0 + y_{2n}) + 4(y_1 + y_3 + \dots + y_{2n-1}) + 2(y_2 + y_4 + \dots + y_{2n-2})\big]
```

- (MEI) T₂ₙ = ½(Mₙ + Tₙ) and S₂ₙ = (2Mₙ + Tₙ)/3 = (4T₂ₙ − Tₙ)/3.
- (MEI) The midpoint and trapezium rules are second order (halving h roughly quarters the error); Simpson's rule is fourth order (halving h divides the error by about 16).

#### Explained

The mid-ordinate rule uses the height at the middle of each strip. Simpson's rule fits a parabola through each set of three points, which is why it needs an even number of strips and is far more accurate.

For a curve that is concave up (y″ > 0), the trapezium rule overestimates and the midpoint rule underestimates; for concave down it is the other way round. The true value lies between them, and Simpson's weighted average is closer still.

#### Worked example

Estimate ∫₀¹ e^{−x²} dx using Simpson's rule with 4 strips.

1. h = 0.25. y₀ = 1, y₁ = 0.939413, y₂ = 0.778801, y₃ = 0.569783, y₄ = 0.367879.
2. S = (0.25/3)[1 + 0.367879 + 4(0.939413 + 0.569783) + 2(0.778801)] = 0.746855.

Same integral: find M₄ and T₄, and hence S₈.

1. M₄ = 0.25[f(0.125) + f(0.375) + f(0.625) + f(0.875)] = 0.748747.
2. T₄ = 0.25[½(1 + 0.367879) + 0.939413 + 0.778801 + 0.569783] = 0.742984.
3. S₈ = (2 × 0.748747 + 0.742984)/3 = 0.746826.

#### Common mistakes

- Using an odd number of strips with Simpson's rule.
- Swapping the 4 and 2 weights.

### J2 Euler's method

*Refs: AQA J2 · Edexcel FP1 6.1 · MEI Tc7, Tc8. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

For dy/dx = f(x, y) with y(x₀) = y₀ and step h:

```latex
y_{r+1} = y_r + h\,f(x_r, y_r),\qquad x_{r+1} = x_r + h
```

- A smaller step usually gives a better answer, but takes more steps and builds up rounding error.

#### Explained

Each step follows the tangent line for a distance h. If the curve bends away from the tangent, the error grows with each step. For a curve that is concave up, Euler underestimates.

#### Worked example

dy/dx = x + y, y(0) = 1. Use Euler's method with h = 0.1 to estimate y(0.2).

1. y₁ = 1 + 0.1(0 + 1) = 1.1.
2. y₂ = 1.1 + 0.1(0.1 + 1.1) = 1.22.
3. The exact value is 2e^{0.2} − 1.2 = 1.24281, so Euler underestimates (the solution is concave up).

#### Common mistakes

- Using f(x_{r+1}, y_{r+1}) in the step.

### J3 Improved and modified Euler methods

*Refs: AQA J3 · Edexcel FP1 6.1 · MEI Tc7, Tc8. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- AQA improved Euler (and Edexcel's central difference): y_{r+1} = y_{r−1} + 2hf(x_r, y_r). It needs two starting values; get y₁ from one Euler step.
- MEI modified Euler: k₁ = hf(xₙ, yₙ), k₂ = hf(xₙ + h, yₙ + k₁), y_{n+1} = yₙ + ½(k₁ + k₂).

#### Explained

Both use more information about the slope than plain Euler. The two-step formula uses the gradient at the middle of a 2h interval. The modified (predictor-corrector) method averages the gradient at the start and at an Euler prediction of the end.

#### Worked example

dy/dx = x + y, y(0) = 1. Use the AQA improved Euler formula with h = 0.1 to estimate y(0.2), taking y₁ from Euler.

1. y₁ = 1.1 (Euler).
2. y₂ = y₀ + 2(0.1)f(0.1, 1.1) = 1 + 0.2(1.2) = 1.24.

Same equation: use the MEI modified Euler method with h = 0.1 for two steps.

1. Step 1: k₁ = 0.1(1) = 0.1, k₂ = 0.1(0.1 + 1.1) = 0.12. y₁ = 1 + 0.11 = 1.11.
2. Step 2: k₁ = 0.1(0.1 + 1.11) = 0.121, k₂ = 0.1(0.2 + 1.231) = 0.1431. y₂ = 1.11 + 0.13205 = 1.24205.

#### Common mistakes

- Using y_r instead of y_{r−1} at the start of the two-step formula.

### Runge-Kutta methods

*Refs: MEI Tc9, Tc10. Level: A-level only (option content).*

#### What you need to know

- The fourth order Runge-Kutta method takes a weighted average of four slope estimates:

```latex
k_1 = hf(x_n, y_n),\ k_2 = hf\!\left(x_n + \tfrac h2, y_n + \tfrac{k_1}{2}\right),\ k_3 = hf\!\left(x_n + \tfrac h2, y_n + \tfrac{k_2}{2}\right),\ k_4 = hf(x_n + h, y_n + k_3)
```

```latex
y_{n+1} = y_n + \tfrac16(k_1 + 2k_2 + 2k_3 + k_4)
```

- Use it with software (a spreadsheet) and compare step lengths.

#### Worked example

dy/dx = x + y, y(0) = 1. One step of RK4 with h = 0.2.

1. k₁ = 0.2(1) = 0.2. k₂ = 0.2(0.1 + 1.1) = 0.24. k₃ = 0.2(0.1 + 1.12) = 0.244. k₄ = 0.2(0.2 + 1.244) = 0.2888.
2. y₁ = 1 + (0.2 + 0.48 + 0.488 + 0.2888)/6 = 1.2428, very close to the exact 1.24281.

#### Common mistakes

- Forgetting to halve h in the middle two slope estimates.

### Second order equations step by step

*Refs: Edexcel FP1 6.1. Level: A-level only (option content).*

#### What you need to know

- (d²y/dx²)ₙ ≈ (y_{n+1} − 2yₙ + y_{n−1})/h², so y_{n+1} ≈ 2yₙ − y_{n−1} + h²(d²y/dx²)ₙ.
- Start with y₁ from y₀ and (dy/dx)₀: y₁ ≈ y₀ + h(dy/dx)₀.

#### Worked example

d²y/dx² = −y, y = 1 and dy/dx = 0 at x = 0. Estimate y at x = 0.2 with h = 0.1.

1. y₁ ≈ 1 + 0.1 × 0 = 1.
2. y₂ ≈ 2(1) − 1 + 0.01(−1) = 0.99. (The exact value is cos 0.2 = 0.98007.)

#### Common mistakes

- Using h instead of h² in the second order formula.

### Check questions

1. Use the mid-ordinate rule with 4 strips to estimate ∫₁³ ln x dx, to 4 decimal places. [4 marks]
2. Use Simpson's rule with 4 strips to estimate ∫₀² √(1 + x³) dx, to 4 decimal places. [4 marks]
3. dy/dx = y² − x, y(0) = 1. Use Euler's method with h = 0.1 to estimate y(0.3). [4 marks]
4. (AQA) dy/dx = xy, y(1) = 2. Use Euler for one step and then the improved Euler formula y_{r+1} = y_{r−1} + 2hf(x_r, y_r) with h = 0.1 to estimate y(1.2). [4 marks]
5. (MEI) Given M₂ = 0.6935 and T₂ = 0.7083 for some integral, find T₄ and S₄. [3 marks]

### Mark scheme

1. h = 0.5; mid-ordinates at 1.25, 1.75, 2.25, 2.75 (1). ln values 0.22314, 0.55962, 0.81093, 1.01160 (1). Sum 2.60529 × 0.5 (1) = 1.3026 (1).
2. h = 0.5; y values 1, 1.06066, 1.41421, 2.09165, 3 (1). (0.5/3)[1 + 3 + 4(1.06066 + 2.09165) + 2(1.41421)] (2) = 3.2396 (1).
3. y₁ = 1 + 0.1(1) = 1.1 (1). y₂ = 1.1 + 0.1(1.21 − 0.1) = 1.211 (1). y₃ = 1.211 + 0.1(1.466521 − 0.2) = 1.33765 (2).
4. y₁ = 2 + 0.1(2) = 2.2 (1). f(1.1, 2.2) = 2.42 (1). y₂ = 2 + 0.2 × 2.42 (1) = 2.484 (1).
5. T₄ = ½(0.6935 + 0.7083) = 0.7009 (1). S₄ = (2 × 0.6935 + 0.7083)/3 (1) = 0.6984 (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/further-maths/16-differential-equations-first-order.md -->

## Differential equations: first order and integrating factors

This file covers general and particular solutions, the integrating factor method for first order linear equations, setting up and interpreting first order models, and (Edexcel FP1) first order equations reduced to a standard type by a substitution.

### Overview

All A-level only. Separable equations and simple modelling are in A-level Maths ([../maths/16-differential-equations.md](../maths/16-differential-equations.md)). Second order equations, oscillations and coupled systems are in [17-differential-equations-second-order-and-oscillations.md](17-differential-equations-second-order-and-oscillations.md). Numerical methods (Euler) are in [22-numerical-integration-and-step-by-step-methods.md](22-numerical-integration-and-step-by-step-methods.md).

#### Board differences

- AQA I1 to I3 (and the modelling themes OT3.1 to OT3.5); Edexcel CP9.1 to CP9.3, FP1 3.2; OCR 4.10a to 4.10c; MEI Pp19 to Pp22, Pc7 to Pc10.
- MEI Pp19 to Pp22 stress forming equations from words, including kinematics language (rate of change of velocity, and so on).
- Edexcel FP1 3.2 covers equations reducible to standard types by a given substitution.

### I2 General and particular solutions

*Refs: AQA I2 · Edexcel CP9.2 · OCR 4.10a · MEI Pc7. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- A general solution of a first order equation has one arbitrary constant and represents a family of curves.
- A particular solution uses a boundary or initial condition to fix the constant.
- Verify a solution by substituting it into the equation.

#### Explained

Sketching a few members of the family (with different constants) shows how the condition picks out one curve. In context, the initial condition is usually the value at t = 0.

#### Worked example

Verify that y = Ax − x² is a solution of x dy/dx − y = −x², and find the particular solution through (1, 3).

1. dy/dx = A − 2x. x(A − 2x) − (Ax − x²) = −x² ✓.
2. 3 = A − 1, A = 4. y = 4x − x².

#### Common mistakes

- Applying the condition before integrating has finished, or forgetting the constant.

### I1 The integrating factor

*Refs: AQA I1 · Edexcel CP9.1 · OCR 4.10c · MEI Pc8, Pc9, Pc10. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- For dy/dx + P(x)y = Q(x), multiply through by

```latex
I(x) = e^{\int P(x)\,dx}
```

- Then the left side is d/dx (Iy), so

```latex
I(x)\,y = \int I(x)\,Q(x)\,dx
```

- Recognise when the method applies: the equation must be linear in y, with coefficient of dy/dx equal to 1 (divide through first).

#### Explained

The integrating factor makes the left side the result of the product rule: d/dx(Iy) = I dy/dx + I′y, and I′ = PI by design. Then integrate both sides.

Steps:

1. Rewrite in standard form, dividing by the coefficient of dy/dx.
2. Find I = e^{∫P dx}. No constant is needed. Simplify: e^{2 ln x} = x².
3. Write Iy = ∫IQ dx + c. Do not forget the constant.
4. Divide by I. Apply any condition.

Some equations can be solved by separating the variables as well; use whichever is easier.

#### Worked example

Solve dy/dx + 2y/x = x for x > 0, given y = 1 when x = 1.

1. P = 2/x, I = e^{2 ln x} = x².
2. x²y = ∫x³ dx = x⁴/4 + c.
3. x = 1: 1 = ¼ + c, c = ¾. y = x²/4 + 3/(4x²).

Solve dy/dx + y tan x = cos²x for −π/2 < x < π/2, given y = 2 when x = 0.

1. I = e^{∫tan x dx} = e^{ln sec x} = sec x.
2. y sec x = ∫cos x dx = sin x + c.
3. x = 0: 2 = c. y = cos x(sin x + 2).

Solve dy/dx − 3y = eˣ, given y = 1 when x = 0.

1. I = e^{−3x}. ye^{−3x} = ∫e^{−2x} dx = −½e^{−2x} + c.
2. x = 0: 1 = −½ + c, c = 3/2.
3. y = −½eˣ + (3/2)e^{3x}.

Find the general solution of x dy/dx − y = x³ for x > 0.

1. Standard form: dy/dx − y/x = x². I = e^{−ln x} = 1/x.
2. y/x = ∫x dx = x²/2 + c. y = x³/2 + cx.

#### Common mistakes

- Using the integrating factor without first dividing by the coefficient of dy/dx.
- Forgetting the constant, or adding it after dividing by I (it must be divided by I too).
- e^{−ln x} = −x (it is 1/x).

### I3 Modelling with first order equations

*Refs: AQA I3, OT3.1 to OT3.5 · Edexcel CP9.3 · OCR 4.10b · MEI Pp19, Pp20, Pp21, Pp22. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- Form an equation from a description: rates of change, proportionality, inflow minus outflow, Newton's second law with a resistance.
- Solve, interpret in context, and comment on the model's limits and possible refinements.

#### Explained

Typical set-ups:

- **Mixing.** Amount of salt S in a tank: dS/dt = (rate in) − (rate out), where rate out = concentration × outflow rate = (S/V) × outflow, and V may change with t.
- **Motion with resistance.** m dv/dt = force − kv. Read "the rate of change of velocity" as dv/dt (MEI Pp21).
- **Newton's law of cooling, growth and decay.** Rate proportional to a difference.

Always state what each variable means and its units. When interpreting, look at what happens as t → ∞ and check whether the answer is realistic. Refine a model by dropping an assumption (for example, that the resistance is proportional to v rather than v²).

#### Worked example

A tank holds 100 litres of pure water. Brine containing 2 g of salt per litre flows in at 1 litre per minute, and the well-mixed solution flows out at 1 litre per minute, so the volume stays 100 litres. Find the mass of salt S(t) grams.

1. dS/dt = 2 × 1 − (S/100) × 1 = 2 − S/100.
2. I = e^{t/100}. Se^{t/100} = ∫2e^{t/100} dt = 200e^{t/100} + c.
3. t = 0, S = 0: c = −200. S = 200(1 − e^{−t/100}).
4. As t → ∞, S → 200 g: the concentration tends to 2 g per litre, the inflow concentration.

In a second tank, brine at 2 g per litre flows in at 3 litres per minute and the mixture flows out at 2 litres per minute. The tank starts with 100 litres of pure water. Find S(t).

1. Volume V = 100 + t. dS/dt = 6 − 2S/(100 + t).
2. I = (100 + t)². d/dt[S(100 + t)²] = 6(100 + t)².
3. S(100 + t)² = 2(100 + t)³ + c. t = 0: c = −2 × 10⁶.
4. S = 2(100 + t) − 2 × 10⁶/(100 + t)².
5. The model fails once the tank is full; its capacity limits t.

A particle of mass 2 kg starts from rest. At time t s the resultant force on it is 2 − 2v/(t + 2) newtons, where v m s⁻¹ is its velocity. Find v.

1. Newton's second law: 2 dv/dt = 2 − 2v/(t + 2), so dv/dt + v/(t + 2) = 1.

2. I = t + 2. v(t + 2) = ∫(t + 2) dt = t²/2 + 2t + c, and v = 0 at t = 0 gives c = 0.
3. v = t(t + 4)/[2(t + 2)].

#### Common mistakes

- Writing the outflow concentration as S instead of S/V.
- Forgetting that V changes when inflow and outflow rates differ.
- Giving no interpretation or limitation when asked.

### Equations reducible by a substitution

*Refs: Edexcel FP1 3.2. Level: A-level only (option content).*

#### What you need to know

- Use a given substitution to turn an equation into a separable or integrating-factor equation, solve, then substitute back.
- Common examples: y = vx for dy/dx = f(y/x); z = y^{1−n} for dy/dx + Py = Qyⁿ.

#### Explained

If y = vx, then dy/dx = v + x dv/dx (product rule). Replace every y and dy/dx, simplify to an equation in v and x, solve, and finally put v = y/x.

#### Worked example

Use y = vx to solve x y dy/dx = x² + y² for x > 0.

1. dy/dx = (x² + y²)/(xy) = 1/v + v.
2. v + x dv/dx = 1/v + v, so x dv/dx = 1/v.
3. Separate: ∫v dv = ∫1/x dx, v²/2 = ln x + c.
4. y² = x²(2 ln x + A).

Use z = 1/y to solve dy/dx + y = xy².

1. y = 1/z, dy/dx = −(1/z²) dz/dx.
2. −(1/z²) dz/dx + 1/z = x/z², so dz/dx − z = −x.
3. I = e^{−x}: ze^{−x} = ∫−xe^{−x} dx = xe^{−x} + e^{−x} + c.
4. z = x + 1 + ceˣ, y = 1/(x + 1 + ceˣ).

#### Common mistakes

- Forgetting the product rule when differentiating y = vx.
- Not substituting back to y at the end.

### Check questions

1. Find the general solution of dy/dx + 3y = e^{2x}. [4 marks]
2. Solve (x + 1) dy/dx + y = 2x for x > −1, given y = 3 when x = 0. [5 marks]
3. Solve cos x dy/dx + y sin x = 1 for 0 ≤ x < π/2, given y = 2 when x = 0. [6 marks]
4. A tank contains 50 litres of water with 100 g of dissolved salt. Pure water flows in at 2 litres per minute and the mixture flows out at 2 litres per minute. (a) Form and solve a differential equation for the salt S g after t minutes. (b) Find when the salt has halved. (c) Give one limitation of the model. [8 marks]
5. A boat of mass 500 kg is moving at 10 m s⁻¹ when its engine stops. The water resistance is 50v N. Find v in terms of t and the distance travelled before v = 1 m s⁻¹. [7 marks]
6. (Edexcel FP1) Use the substitution y = vx to show that the equation x dy/dx = y − x becomes x dv/dx = −1. Hence solve it, given y = 2 when x = 1. [5 marks]

### Mark scheme

1. I = e^{3x} (1). ye^{3x} = ∫e^{5x} dx = e^{5x}/5 + c (2). y = e^{2x}/5 + ce^{−3x} (1).
2. Left side is d/dx[(x + 1)y] (1). (x + 1)y = x² + c (2). c = 3 (1). y = (x² + 3)/(x + 1) (1).
3. dy/dx + y tan x = sec x (1). I = sec x (1). y sec x = ∫sec²x dx = tan x + c (2). c = 2 (1). y = sin x + 2 cos x (1).
4. (a) dS/dt = −2S/50 = −S/25 (2). S = 100e^{−t/25} (2). (b) 50 = 100e^{−t/25}, t = 25 ln 2 = 17.3 minutes (2). (c) E.g. mixing is not instant (1); the flow rates may not stay constant (1).
5. 500 dv/dt = −50v (1), dv/dt = −v/10 (1), v = 10e^{−t/10} (1). v = 1 at t = 10 ln 10 (1). Distance ∫₀^{10 ln 10} 10e^{−t/10} dt (1) = 100(1 − 1/10) (1) = 90 m (1).
6. v + x dv/dx = v − 1 (2), so x dv/dx = −1 (1). v = −ln x + c, y = x(c − ln x) (1). c = 2: y = x(2 − ln x) (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/further-maths/02-complex-numbers-cartesian-form-and-polynomial-roots.md -->

## Complex numbers: Cartesian form, conjugates and roots of polynomials

This file covers the number i, complex numbers written as x + iy, their arithmetic, complex conjugates, square roots of complex numbers, and solving quadratic, cubic and quartic equations with real coefficients.

### Overview

All AS content on every board. It assumes the quadratic formula, the discriminant, the factor theorem and polynomial division from A-level Maths ([../maths/02-algebra-indices-surds-quadratics-and-inequalities.md](../maths/02-algebra-indices-surds-quadratics-and-inequalities.md), [../maths/03-algebra-polynomials-and-partial-fractions.md](../maths/03-algebra-polynomials-and-partial-fractions.md)). The geometry of complex numbers (Argand diagrams, modulus and argument) is in [03-complex-numbers-argand-diagrams-modulus-argument-and-loci.md](03-complex-numbers-argand-diagrams-modulus-argument-and-loci.md).

#### Board differences

- AQA B1 to B3; Edexcel CP2.1 to CP2.3; OCR 4.02a to 4.02c, 4.02e, 4.02g to 4.02j; MEI Pj1 to Pj5.
- OCR 4.02h names finding the two square roots of a complex number algebraically. The other boards use the same skill when solving quadratics with complex coefficients is not required, so treat it as useful for all.
- AQA B1 and Edexcel CP2.1 say a cubic will come with enough information to find one root, and a quartic with one complex root or one quadratic factor.

### B2 The language and arithmetic of complex numbers

*Refs: AQA B2 · Edexcel CP2.2 · OCR 4.02a, 4.02b, 4.02c, 4.02e · MEI Pj1, Pj4, Pj5. Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- i² = −1, so √(−k) = i√k for k > 0.
- A complex number z = x + iy, with x, y real. Re(z) = x is the real part, Im(z) = y is the imaginary part (a real number, not iy).
- ℂ is the set of complex numbers. Real numbers are the complex numbers with y = 0; purely imaginary numbers have x = 0.
- Two complex numbers are equal if and only if their real parts are equal and their imaginary parts are equal. So z = 0 if and only if x = 0 and y = 0.
- Add, subtract, multiply and divide complex numbers.

#### Explained

Treat i like an algebraic letter, and replace i² by −1 whenever it appears. Powers of i repeat in a cycle of four: i, −1, −i, 1.

```latex
(a+bi)(c+di) = (ac-bd) + (ad+bc)i
```

To divide, multiply the top and bottom by the conjugate of the bottom. This makes the denominator real, because (c + di)(c − di) = c² + d².

```latex
\frac{a+bi}{c+di} = \frac{(a+bi)(c-di)}{c^2+d^2}
```

Equating real and imaginary parts turns one complex equation into two real ones. This is how you solve for unknown real numbers.

#### Worked example

Simplify (3 − 2i)(4 + 5i).

1. 12 + 15i − 8i − 10i² = 12 + 7i + 10 = 22 + 7i.

Write (5 + i)/(2 − 3i) in the form x + iy.

1. Multiply top and bottom by 2 + 3i.
2. Top: (5 + i)(2 + 3i) = 10 + 15i + 2i + 3i² = 7 + 17i.
3. Bottom: 4 + 9 = 13.
4. Answer: 7/13 + (17/13)i.

Find the real numbers a and b such that (a + bi)(2 + i) = 1 + 8i.

1. Expand: 2a − b + (a + 2b)i.
2. Real: 2a − b = 1. Imaginary: a + 2b = 8.
3. Solve: a = 2, b = 3.

Find i¹⁰³.

1. 103 = 4 × 25 + 3, so i¹⁰³ = i³ = −i.

#### Common mistakes

- Writing Im(3 − 2i) = −2i. It is −2.
- Forgetting that i² = −1 gives a sign change: (2i)² = −4, not 4.
- Dividing the real and imaginary parts separately: (a + bi)/(c + di) is not a/c + (b/d)i.

### B3 The complex conjugate

*Refs: AQA B3 · Edexcel CP2.3 · OCR 4.02c, 4.02g · MEI Pj3. Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- The conjugate of z = x + iy is z* = x − iy.
- zz* = x² + y², a real number. z + z* = 2x, also real.
- (z + w)* = z* + w*, (zw)* = z*w*.
- For a polynomial with real coefficients, non-real roots come in conjugate pairs: if α is a root, so is α*.

#### Explained

Taking the conjugate of every term of p(z) = 0 gives p(z*) = 0 when every coefficient is real, because the conjugate of a real coefficient is itself. So the conjugate of a root is also a root.

A conjugate pair α, α* gives the real quadratic factor

```latex
(z-\alpha)(z-\alpha^*) = z^2 - (\alpha+\alpha^*)z + \alpha\alpha^* = z^2 - 2\,\mathrm{Re}(\alpha)\,z + |\alpha|^2
```

This is the key to cubics and quartics: one complex root gives a whole quadratic factor.

#### Worked example

Solve z + 2z* = 6 − 4i.

1. Let z = x + iy. Then x + iy + 2x − 2iy = 3x − iy.
2. Real: 3x = 6, x = 2. Imaginary: −y = −4, y = 4.
3. z = 2 + 4i.

Find the quadratic with real coefficients that has 3 − 2i as a root.

1. The other root is 3 + 2i. Sum = 6, product = 9 + 4 = 13.
2. z² − 6z + 13 = 0.

Show that z = 1 + i satisfies z² − 2z + 2 = 0, and write down the other root.

1. (1 + i)² = 1 + 2i − 1 = 2i. 2i − 2(1 + i) + 2 = 0. ✓
2. The coefficients are real, so the other root is 1 − i.

#### Common mistakes

- Using the conjugate pair rule when the coefficients are not all real. For z² − iz + 2 = 0, the roots are 2i and −i, not a conjugate pair.
- Writing (z − α)(z − α*) and expanding with sign errors. Use sum and product.

### B1 Solving quadratic, cubic and quartic equations

*Refs: AQA B1 · Edexcel CP2.1 · OCR 4.02g, 4.02h, 4.02i, 4.02j · MEI Pj2, Pj3. Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- Solve any quadratic with real coefficients. A negative discriminant gives a conjugate pair.
- Find the two square roots of a complex number by equating parts.
- Solve a cubic or quartic with real coefficients given one root (or a factor), using conjugate pairs and the factor theorem.

#### Explained

**Quadratics.** Use the formula or complete the square. If b² − 4ac < 0, write √(b² − 4ac) = i√(4ac − b²).

**Square roots of a + bi.** Let (x + iy)² = a + bi. Then x² − y² = a and 2xy = b. Substitute y = b/(2x) into the first and solve the quadratic in x². Only take the positive value of x² (x is real). The two roots are negatives of each other.

**Cubics.** A real cubic has either three real roots or one real root and a conjugate pair. Given a complex root, build the quadratic factor from the pair, then divide (or compare coefficients) to find the linear factor. Given a real root, factorise and solve the quadratic.

**Quartics.** Given one complex root, use its conjugate to get a quadratic factor; the other factor is a quadratic you can solve. Given a quadratic factor, divide.

Comparing coefficients is often quicker than long division. If z³ + pz² + qz + r = (z² + bz + c)(z + d), the constant terms give r = cd.

#### Worked example

Solve z² + 4z + 13 = 0.

1. z = [−4 ± √(16 − 52)]/2 = [−4 ± 6i]/2 = −2 ± 3i.

Find the square roots of 5 − 12i.

1. (x + iy)² = 5 − 12i, so x² − y² = 5 and xy = −6.
2. y = −6/x: x² − 36/x² = 5, so x⁴ − 5x² − 36 = 0, (x² − 9)(x² + 4) = 0.
3. x² = 9, x = ±3, y = ∓2.
4. Square roots: 3 − 2i and −3 + 2i.

Given that 2 + i is a root of z³ − 7z² + 17z − 15 = 0, solve the equation.

1. 2 − i is also a root. Quadratic factor: z² − 4z + 5.
2. z³ − 7z² + 17z − 15 = (z² − 4z + 5)(z − 3). Check the constant: 5 × (−3) = −15. ✓
3. Roots: 2 + i, 2 − i, 3.

Given that 1 − 2i is a root of z⁴ − 2z³ + 6z² − 2z + 5 = 0, solve it.

1. 1 + 2i is a root, so z² − 2z + 5 is a factor.
2. Let z⁴ − 2z³ + 6z² − 2z + 5 = (z² − 2z + 5)(z² + az + b).
3. Constant: 5b = 5, b = 1. z³: a − 2 = −2, a = 0. Check z²: b − 2a + 5 = 6 ✓.
4. z² + 1 = 0 gives z = ±i.
5. Roots: 1 ± 2i, ±i.

Solve z³ + 2z² + 5z − 26 = 0, given z = 2 is a root.

1. Divide: z³ + 2z² + 5z − 26 = (z − 2)(z² + 4z + 13).
2. z² + 4z + 13 = 0 gives z = −2 ± 3i.
3. Roots: 2, −2 ± 3i.

#### Common mistakes

- When finding square roots, accepting x² = −4. x is real, so x² cannot be negative.
- Stopping at the quadratic factor and not finding the remaining roots.
- Forgetting that a quartic has four roots (counted with repetition).

### Check questions

1. Given z = 4 − 3i and w = 1 + 2i, find in the form a + bi: (a) zw, (b) z/w, (c) z*w. [5 marks]
2. Find the real numbers x and y such that (x + iy)(3 − i) = 7 + i. [3 marks]
3. Solve the equation 2z − iz* = 3 + 6i. [4 marks]
4. Find the two square roots of −15 + 8i. [4 marks]
5. Solve z² − 6z + 34 = 0. [2 marks]
6. Given that 3 − i is a root of z³ − 8z² + 22z − 20 = 0, find the other roots. [5 marks]
7. The equation z⁴ − 4z³ + 9z² − 16z + 20 = 0 has a root 2i. Find all the roots. [6 marks]
8. The cubic z³ + az² + bz + 10 = 0, with a and b real, has a root 1 + 2i. Find a and b, and the real root. [6 marks]

### Mark scheme

1. (a) 4 + 8i − 3i − 6i² = 10 + 5i (2). (b) (4 − 3i)(1 − 2i)/5 = (4 − 8i − 3i − 6)/5 = (−2 − 11i)/5 (2). (c) (4 + 3i)(1 + 2i) = 4 + 8i + 3i − 6 = −2 + 11i (1).
2. 3x + y = 7 (1), 3y − x = 1 (1), x = 2, y = 1 (1).
3. z = x + iy: 2x + 2iy − i(x − iy) = 2x − y + i(2y − x) (1). 2x − y = 3, 2y − x = 6 (1). x = 4 (1), y = 5, z = 4 + 5i (1).
4. x² − y² = −15, xy = 4 (1). x⁴ + 15x² − 16 = 0 (1). x² = 1 (1). Roots 1 + 4i and −1 − 4i (1).
5. z = 3 ± 5i (2).
6. 3 + i is a root (1). Factor z² − 6z + 10 (2). Other factor z − 2 (1). Roots 3 + i, 2 (1).
7. −2i is a root (1). Factor z² + 4 (1). Other factor z² + az + 5 with a = −4 (2). z² − 4z + 5 = 0 gives 2 ± i (1). Roots ±2i, 2 ± i (1).
8. 1 − 2i is a root (1). Factor z² − 2z + 5 (1). Real root: constant 5 × (−r) = 10, so the factor is z + 2 and the real root is −2 (1). (z² − 2z + 5)(z + 2) = z³ + 0z² + z + 10 (2). a = 0, b = 1 (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/further-maths/03-complex-numbers-argand-diagrams-modulus-argument-and-loci.md -->

## Complex numbers: Argand diagrams, modulus-argument form and loci

This file covers Argand diagrams, modulus and argument, the modulus-argument form, multiplying and dividing in that form, and loci and regions in the Argand diagram.

### Overview

AS content on every board. You need radians and the compound angle formulae from A-level Maths ([../maths/09-trigonometry-functions-rules-and-radians.md](../maths/09-trigonometry-functions-rules-and-radians.md), [../maths/10-trigonometry-identities-and-equations.md](../maths/10-trigonometry-identities-and-equations.md)) and circles ([../maths/05-coordinate-geometry-lines-and-circles.md](../maths/05-coordinate-geometry-lines-and-circles.md)). Cartesian arithmetic is in [02-complex-numbers-cartesian-form-and-polynomial-roots.md](02-complex-numbers-cartesian-form-and-polynomial-roots.md). Harder loci and transformations of the plane (Edexcel FP2) are in [27-complex-loci-and-transformations.md](27-complex-loci-and-transformations.md).

#### Board differences

- AQA B4 to B7; Edexcel CP2.4 to CP2.7; OCR 4.02f, 4.02k to 4.02m, 4.02o, 4.02p; MEI Pj6 to Pj11, Pj18.
- OCR 4.02p asks for set notation for loci and regions, for example {z : |z − 2| < 3}. OCR 4.02m (Stage 2) is the geometric effect of multiplying by a complex number.
- All boards use the principal argument −π < θ ≤ π. MEI accepts 0 ≤ θ < 2π if stated, but −π < θ ≤ π is the default.
- Edexcel CP2.7 includes regions (inequalities); AQA B7 gives |z − a| > r and arg(z − a) = θ as its examples.

### B4 Argand diagrams

*Refs: AQA B4 · Edexcel CP2.4 · OCR 4.02k, 4.02l · MEI Pj7, Pj9, Pj10. Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- The Argand diagram plots z = x + iy as the point (x, y), or the vector from the origin to (x, y). The horizontal axis is real, the vertical axis imaginary.
- z₁ + z₂ is the vector sum (parallelogram rule); z₁ − z₂ is the vector from z₂ to z₁.
- z* is the reflection of z in the real axis. −z is a half turn about the origin.

#### Explained

Seeing complex numbers as vectors makes loci easy. The distance between the points z₁ and z₂ is |z₁ − z₂|, because z₁ − z₂ is the vector joining them. Roots of a real polynomial are symmetric about the real axis, because they come in conjugate pairs.

#### Worked example

z₁ = 2 + 3i, z₂ = 4 − i. Describe the points z₁ + z₂ and z₁ − z₂ and find the distance between z₁ and z₂.

1. z₁ + z₂ = 6 + 2i, the fourth vertex of the parallelogram with sides 0z₁ and 0z₂.
2. z₁ − z₂ = −2 + 4i, the vector from z₂ to z₁.
3. Distance = |−2 + 4i| = √20 = 2√5.

#### Common mistakes

- Plotting the imaginary part as iy on the axis. The axis is labelled Im, and the point is (x, y).

### B5 Modulus, argument and the modulus-argument form

*Refs: AQA B5 · Edexcel CP2.5 · OCR 4.02c, 4.02f · MEI Pj6, Pj7. Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- The modulus |z| = r = √(x² + y²) is the distance from the origin.
- The argument arg z = θ is the angle from the positive real axis, anticlockwise positive, with −π < θ ≤ π (principal argument). arg 0 is undefined.
- z = r(cos θ + i sin θ), the modulus-argument form, with x = r cos θ, y = r sin θ.

#### Explained

To find the argument, always sketch z first. Find the acute angle α = arctan|y/x|, then:

| Quadrant | arg z |
| --- | --- |
| first (x > 0, y > 0) | α |
| second (x < 0, y > 0) | π − α |
| third (x < 0, y < 0) | −(π − α) |
| fourth (x > 0, y < 0) | −α |

Points on the axes: arg of a positive real is 0, negative real π, positive imaginary π/2, negative imaginary −π/2.

The modulus-argument form must have a plus sign and r > 0. If you are given 3(cos θ − i sin θ), rewrite it as 3(cos(−θ) + i sin(−θ)).

#### Worked example

Write z = −√3 + i in modulus-argument form.

1. r = √(3 + 1) = 2.
2. Second quadrant. α = arctan(1/√3) = π/6, so θ = π − π/6 = 5π/6.
3. z = 2(cos 5π/6 + i sin 5π/6).

Find |z| and arg z for z = −3 − 4i, giving the argument to 3 s.f.

1. |z| = 5.
2. Third quadrant. α = arctan(4/3) = 0.9273. θ = −(π − 0.9273) = −2.21.

Write 4(cos π/3 − i sin π/3) in modulus-argument form, and in the form x + iy.

1. = 4(cos(−π/3) + i sin(−π/3)), so r = 4, θ = −π/3.
2. x + iy = 4(½) − 4(√3/2)i = 2 − 2√3 i.

#### Common mistakes

- Using arctan(y/x) from the calculator without a sketch. For −3 − 4i it gives 0.927, which is the wrong quadrant.
- Giving an argument outside −π < θ ≤ π, such as 5π/3 instead of −π/3.

### B6 Multiplying and dividing in modulus-argument form

*Refs: AQA B6 · Edexcel CP2.6 · OCR 4.02l, 4.02m · MEI Pj8, Pj18. Level: AS & A (MEI Pj18 A-level only).*

#### What you need to know

```latex
|z_1z_2| = |z_1||z_2|,\quad \arg(z_1z_2) = \arg z_1 + \arg z_2
```

```latex
\left|\frac{z_1}{z_2}\right| = \frac{|z_1|}{|z_2|},\quad \arg\frac{z_1}{z_2} = \arg z_1 - \arg z_2
```

- Adjust the resulting argument by ±2π to keep it in −π < θ ≤ π.
- Multiplying by z = r(cos θ + i sin θ) is an enlargement by scale factor r and a rotation by θ about the origin.

#### Explained

The proof uses the compound angle formulae:

```latex
r_1(\cos\theta_1+i\sin\theta_1)\,r_2(\cos\theta_2+i\sin\theta_2) = r_1r_2[\cos(\theta_1+\theta_2)+i\sin(\theta_1+\theta_2)]
```

because the real part is cos θ₁cos θ₂ − sin θ₁sin θ₂ and the imaginary part is sin θ₁cos θ₂ + cos θ₁sin θ₂. Multiplying by i (modulus 1, argument π/2) rotates by 90° anticlockwise.

#### Worked example

z₁ = 6(cos 3π/4 + i sin 3π/4), z₂ = 2(cos π/2 + i sin π/2). Find z₁z₂ and z₁/z₂ in modulus-argument form.

1. z₁z₂: modulus 12, argument 3π/4 + π/2 = 5π/4. Subtract 2π: −3π/4. z₁z₂ = 12(cos(−3π/4) + i sin(−3π/4)).
2. z₁/z₂: modulus 3, argument 3π/4 − π/2 = π/4. z₁/z₂ = 3(cos π/4 + i sin π/4).

Given |z| = 2 and arg z = π/6, find |z³|, arg z³ and arg(1/z).

1. |z³| = 8. arg z³ = π/2.
2. 1/z has modulus ½ and argument −π/6.

The triangle with vertices 0, 1 + i and 3 is mapped by multiplication by 2i. Describe the image.

1. 2i has modulus 2 and argument π/2: enlargement scale factor 2, rotation 90° anticlockwise about 0.
2. Images: 0, 2i(1 + i) = −2 + 2i, 6i. The area is multiplied by 2² = 4.

#### Common mistakes

- Multiplying arguments instead of adding them.
- Forgetting to bring the final argument back into the principal range.

### B7 Loci and regions in the Argand diagram

*Refs: AQA B7 · Edexcel CP2.7 · OCR 4.02o, 4.02p · MEI Pj9, Pj10, Pj11. Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- |z − a| = r is a circle, centre a, radius r. |z − a| < r is the inside (dashed boundary); ≤ includes the boundary (solid).
- |z − a| = |z − b| is the perpendicular bisector of the line joining a and b.
- arg(z − a) = θ is a half-line from a (not including a) at angle θ to the positive real direction.
- Regions combine these, for example |z − 1| ≤ 2 and 0 ≤ arg z ≤ π/4.
- (OCR) Write loci and regions in set notation: {z : |z − 1 − i| ≤ 2}.

#### Explained

Read |z − a| as "the distance from z to a", and arg(z − a) as "the direction from a to z". Watch the signs: |z + 2 − i| = |z − (−2 + i)|, so the centre is −2 + i.

For a half-line, the start point is an open circle because arg 0 is undefined.

To find extreme values over a circle, use geometry. If z lies on |z − c| = r, the largest |z| is |c| + r and the smallest is ||c| − r|, along the line through 0 and c. The largest and smallest arg z come from the tangents to the circle from the origin.

#### Worked example

Sketch |z − 3 + 2i| = 2 and find the greatest and least values of |z| on it.

1. |z − (3 − 2i)| = 2: circle centre (3, −2), radius 2.
2. Distance from origin to centre = √13.
3. Greatest |z| = √13 + 2 ≈ 5.61; least |z| = √13 − 2 ≈ 1.61.

Find the Cartesian equation of the locus |z − 4| = |z + 2i|.

1. With z = x + iy: (x − 4)² + y² = x² + (y + 2)².
2. −8x + 16 = 4y + 4, so y = −2x + 3.
3. This is the perpendicular bisector of (4, 0) and (0, −2).

Shade the region where |z − 2i| ≤ 2 and π/4 ≤ arg z ≤ π/2, and find the point in the region with the largest real part.

1. The circle has centre (0, 2), radius 2, touching the origin. The region is the part of the disc between the half-lines y = x (x > 0) and the positive imaginary axis.
2. The line y = x meets the circle where x² + (x − 2)² = 4, so 2x² − 4x = 0, x = 2.
3. The point 2 + 2i is in the region and on both boundaries; the disc's rightmost point (2, 2) is that same point. Largest real part 2, at z = 2 + 2i.

Find the complex numbers satisfying both arg(z + 1) = π/4 and |z| = 5.

1. The half-line: y = x + 1 with x > −1.
2. x² + (x + 1)² = 25: 2x² + 2x − 24 = 0, x² + x − 12 = 0, x = 3 or −4.
3. x = −4 is not on the half-line (x > −1 needed). So z = 3 + 4i.

#### Common mistakes

- Taking the centre of |z + 3 − i| = 2 as (3, −1). It is (−3, 1).
- Drawing arg(z − a) = θ as a full line through a.
- Using a solid boundary for a strict inequality.

### Check questions

1. z = 1 − √3 i. Find |z| and arg z, and write z in modulus-argument form. [3 marks]
2. z = 2(cos 2π/3 + i sin 2π/3) and w = √2(cos(−π/4) + i sin(−π/4)). Find zw and z/w in modulus-argument form. [4 marks]
3. Find, in the form x + iy, the number with modulus 6 and argument −5π/6. [2 marks]
4. Sketch on one Argand diagram the loci |z + 1 − 2i| = 3 and arg(z + 1) = π/2, and find the complex number satisfying both. [5 marks]
5. Find the Cartesian equation of the locus |z − 2 − i| = |z − 4 + i|, and describe it. [4 marks]
6. Shade the region R = {z : |z − 4 − 4i| ≤ 2√2} and find the greatest and least values of arg z for z in R. [5 marks]
7. The point P represents z, where |z − 5i| = 3. Find the greatest value of |z| and the value of z where it occurs. [3 marks]
8. Multiplication by w maps 1 to 1 + √3 i. Describe the transformation, and find the image of 2 − 2i. [4 marks]

### Mark scheme

1. |z| = 2 (1). arg z = −π/3 (1). z = 2(cos(−π/3) + i sin(−π/3)) (1).
2. |zw| = 2√2, arg = 2π/3 − π/4 = 5π/12 (2). |z/w| = √2, arg = 2π/3 + π/4 = 11π/12 (2).
3. 6(cos(−5π/6) + i sin(−5π/6)) = −3√3 − 3i (2).
4. Circle centre (−1, 2) radius 3 (1); half-line from (−1, 0) upward, open endpoint (1). On x = −1: (y − 2)² = 9 (1), y = 5 or −1 (1); y > 0 needed, so z = −1 + 5i (1).
5. (x − 2)² + (y − 1)² = (x − 4)² + (y + 1)² (1). −4x − 2y + 5 = −8x + 2y + 17 (1). y = x − 3 (1). Perpendicular bisector of (2, 1) and (4, −1) (1).
6. Disc centre (4, 4), radius 2√2, including boundary (1). Centre at distance 4√2 from origin, arg π/4 (1). Half-angle of tangents: sin α = 2√2/(4√2) = ½, α = π/6 (1). Greatest π/4 + π/6 = 5π/12 (1), least π/4 − π/6 = π/12 (1).
7. Centre 5i at distance 5 from 0 (1). Greatest |z| = 8 (1), at z = 8i (1).
8. w = 1 + √3 i: modulus 2, argument π/3 (1). Enlargement scale factor 2 and rotation π/3 anticlockwise about 0 (1). (2 − 2i)(1 + √3 i) = 2 + 2√3 i − 2i + 2√3 (1) = (2 + 2√3) + (2√3 − 2)i (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/further-maths/04-complex-numbers-de-moivre-exponential-form-and-nth-roots.md -->

## Complex numbers: de Moivre's theorem, exponential form and nth roots

This file covers the exponential form re^{iθ}, de Moivre's theorem and its proof, multiple angle formulae, powers of sines and cosines, sums of series, nth roots of complex numbers, and roots of unity in geometry.

### Overview

All A-level only. It builds on modulus-argument form in [03-complex-numbers-argand-diagrams-modulus-argument-and-loci.md](03-complex-numbers-argand-diagrams-modulus-argument-and-loci.md), the binomial expansion ([../maths/07-binomial-expansion.md](../maths/07-binomial-expansion.md)) and geometric series ([../maths/08-sequences-and-series.md](../maths/08-sequences-and-series.md)). Proof by induction is in [01-proof-by-induction.md](01-proof-by-induction.md).

#### Board differences

- AQA B8 to B11; Edexcel CP2.8 to CP2.11; OCR 4.02d, 4.02n, 4.02q to 4.02s; MEI Pj12 to Pj20.
- OCR 4.02d places the exponential form at Stage 2; MEI Pj14 the same. Edexcel CP2.8 names proving de Moivre's theorem by induction for positive integers; MEI Pp5 gives it as an induction example.
- MEI Pj17 asks you to explain why the sum of all nth roots is zero.

### B9 The exponential form

*Refs: AQA B9 · Edexcel CP2.9 · OCR 4.02d, 4.02n · MEI Pj14. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

```latex
e^{i\theta} = \cos\theta + i\sin\theta, \qquad z = re^{i\theta}
```

- r e^{iθ} × s e^{iφ} = rs e^{i(θ+φ)}, and r e^{iθ} ÷ s e^{iφ} = (r/s) e^{i(θ−φ)}.
- The conjugate of re^{iθ} is re^{−iθ}.
- cos θ = (e^{iθ} + e^{−iθ})/2 and sin θ = (e^{iθ} − e^{−iθ})/(2i).

#### Explained

Euler's relation can be seen from Maclaurin series ([09-maclaurin-series-and-limits.md](09-maclaurin-series-and-limits.md)): put x = iθ into the series for eˣ and group the real and imaginary terms; they are the series for cos θ and sin θ. The exponential form makes the rules for multiplying and dividing just the laws of indices. A famous case: e^{iπ} = −1.

#### Worked example

Write z = −2 − 2i in the form re^{iθ}.

1. r = 2√2. Third quadrant, θ = −3π/4.
2. z = 2√2 e^{−3πi/4}.

Given z = 4e^{iπ/3} and w = 2e^{−iπ/2}, find zw and z/w in exponential form.

1. zw = 8e^{−iπ/6}.
2. z/w = 2e^{5iπ/6}.

Show that e^{iθ} + e^{−iθ} = 2 cos θ.

1. e^{iθ} = cos θ + i sin θ, e^{−iθ} = cos θ − i sin θ, since cos is even and sin is odd.
2. Adding: 2 cos θ.

#### Common mistakes

- Writing e^{iθ} with θ in degrees. θ is in radians.
- Taking e^{−iθ} as −e^{iθ}.

### B8 De Moivre's theorem

*Refs: AQA B8 · Edexcel CP2.8 · OCR 4.02q · MEI Pj12, Pj13. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

```latex
[r(\cos\theta + i\sin\theta)]^n = r^n(\cos n\theta + i\sin n\theta)
```

- True for all integers n (and proved by induction for positive n).
- Use it to find powers, to write cos nθ and sin nθ in powers of cos θ and sin θ, to write powers like cos⁴θ as sums of multiple angles, and to sum series.

#### Explained

**Proof by induction for positive integers.** For n = 1 it is clear. Assume (cos θ + i sin θ)ᵏ = cos kθ + i sin kθ. Multiplying by cos θ + i sin θ and using the multiplication rule gives cos(k + 1)θ + i sin(k + 1)θ. For negative n = −m, use 1/(cos mθ + i sin mθ) = cos mθ − i sin mθ = cos(−mθ) + i sin(−mθ).

**Multiple angles.** Expand (c + is)ⁿ, where c = cos θ, s = sin θ, by the binomial theorem. The real part is cos nθ and the imaginary part is sin nθ.

**Powers of sin and cos.** With z = e^{iθ}:

```latex
z^n + z^{-n} = 2\cos n\theta, \qquad z^n - z^{-n} = 2i\sin n\theta
```

So 2 cos θ = z + 1/z. Expand (z + 1/z)ⁿ, pair terms zᵏ with z^{−k}, and replace each pair.

**Sums of series.** Write a sum of cosines as the real part of a geometric series in e^{iθ}, sum the series, and take the real part. Multiplying top and bottom by a conjugate, or taking out a half-angle factor like e^{iθ/2}, makes the real part easy to see.

#### Worked example

Find (1 + i)¹⁰ in the form a + bi.

1. 1 + i = √2 e^{iπ/4}.
2. (1 + i)¹⁰ = 32 e^{10iπ/4} = 32 e^{5iπ/2} = 32 e^{iπ/2} = 32i.

Express cos 3θ and sin 3θ in terms of cos θ and sin θ.

1. (c + is)³ = c³ + 3c²(is) + 3c(is)² + (is)³ = c³ − 3cs² + i(3c²s − s³).
2. cos 3θ = c³ − 3cs² = c³ − 3c(1 − c²) = 4c³ − 3c.
3. sin 3θ = 3c²s − s³ = 3(1 − s²)s − s³ = 3s − 4s³.

Show that cos⁴θ = (cos 4θ + 4 cos 2θ + 3)/8, and hence find ∫₀^{π/2} cos⁴θ dθ.

1. (2 cos θ)⁴ = (z + 1/z)⁴ = z⁴ + 4z² + 6 + 4z⁻² + z⁻⁴.
2. = (z⁴ + z⁻⁴) + 4(z² + z⁻²) + 6 = 2 cos 4θ + 8 cos 2θ + 6.
3. 16 cos⁴θ = 2 cos 4θ + 8 cos 2θ + 6, so cos⁴θ = (cos 4θ + 4 cos 2θ + 3)/8.
4. ∫₀^{π/2}: [sin 4θ/4 + 2 sin 2θ + 3θ]/8 from 0 to π/2 = (3π/2)/8 = 3π/16.

Express sin⁵θ in terms of multiple angles.

1. (2i sin θ)⁵ = (z − 1/z)⁵ = z⁵ − 5z³ + 10z − 10z⁻¹ + 5z⁻³ − z⁻⁵.
2. = (z⁵ − z⁻⁵) − 5(z³ − z⁻³) + 10(z − z⁻¹) = 2i sin 5θ − 10i sin 3θ + 20i sin θ.
3. (2i)⁵ = 32i, so sin⁵θ = (sin 5θ − 5 sin 3θ + 10 sin θ)/16.

Find Σ_{k=0}^{n−1} cos kθ, for θ not a multiple of 2π.

1. It is the real part of Σ e^{ikθ} = (e^{inθ} − 1)/(e^{iθ} − 1).
2. Take out half angles: (e^{inθ/2}/e^{iθ/2}) × (e^{inθ/2} − e^{−inθ/2})/(e^{iθ/2} − e^{−iθ/2}) = e^{i(n−1)θ/2} × sin(nθ/2)/sin(θ/2).
3. Real part: cos((n − 1)θ/2) sin(nθ/2)/sin(θ/2).

#### Common mistakes

- Expanding (c + is)ⁿ and forgetting that i² = −1 and i³ = −i.
- In the z + 1/z method, forgetting to divide by 2ⁿ (or (2i)ⁿ) at the end.
- Applying de Moivre to r(cos θ − i sin θ) without first rewriting with −θ.

### B10 nth roots of a complex number

*Refs: AQA B10 · Edexcel CP2.10 · OCR 4.02r · MEI Pj15, Pj16, Pj17, Pj19. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- Every non-zero complex number has exactly n distinct nth roots.

```latex
z^n = re^{i\theta} \;\Rightarrow\; z = r^{1/n}e^{i(\theta + 2k\pi)/n},\quad k = 0, 1, \dots, n-1
```

- The roots lie on a circle of radius r^{1/n}, equally spaced by 2π/n: they are the vertices of a regular n-gon centred at the origin.
- The nth roots of unity are 1, ω, ω², ..., ω^{n−1}, with ω = e^{2πi/n}. Their sum is 0.

#### Explained

Write the right side with a general argument θ + 2kπ, because adding 2π does not change the number but does change θ/n. Pick k so the arguments lie in −π < arg ≤ π.

Why the sum of the roots of unity is zero: 1 + ω + ... + ω^{n−1} = (ωⁿ − 1)/(ω − 1) = 0 because ωⁿ = 1 and ω ≠ 1. Equivalently, zⁿ − 1 has no z^{n−1} term, so the sum of roots is 0 (file 07).

Once one nth root α of w is known, all of them are α, αω, αω², ..., since (αωᵏ)ⁿ = αⁿ = w.

#### Worked example

Solve z³ = 8i, giving the roots in the form re^{iθ} and x + iy.

1. 8i = 8e^{i(π/2 + 2kπ)}. z = 2e^{i(π/6 + 2kπ/3)}.
2. k = 0: 2e^{iπ/6} = √3 + i. k = 1: 2e^{5iπ/6} = −√3 + i. k = −1: 2e^{−iπ/2} = −2i.

Solve z⁴ = −16.

1. −16 = 16e^{i(π + 2kπ)}. z = 2e^{i(π/4 + kπ/2)}.
2. k = 0, 1, −1, −2: arguments π/4, 3π/4, −π/4, −3π/4.
3. z = ±√2 ± √2 i (all four sign combinations).

Solve z⁵ = 1 and show the roots on an Argand diagram.

1. z = e^{2kπi/5}, k = −2, −1, 0, 1, 2.
2. They are the vertices of a regular pentagon on the unit circle, one vertex at 1.

#### Common mistakes

- Giving only one root, or giving n roots that are not all distinct (k = 0 and k = n repeat).
- Taking the nth root of the argument but not the modulus, or vice versa.

### B11 Roots of unity and geometry

*Refs: AQA B11 · Edexcel CP2.11 · OCR 4.02s · MEI Pj17, Pj18, Pj20. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- Use roots of unity to find the vertices of regular polygons with a given centre and one given vertex.
- Use 1 + ω + ω² + ... + ω^{n−1} = 0 to simplify expressions and prove trigonometric results.

#### Explained

A regular n-gon with centre c and one vertex p has vertices c + (p − c)ωᵏ: each multiplication by ω rotates the vector from the centre by 2π/n.

Taking real parts of the sum of roots gives identities such as cos(2π/5) + cos(4π/5) = −½.

#### Worked example

An equilateral triangle has centre 1 + i and one vertex 3 + i. Find the other vertices.

1. p − c = 2. ω = e^{2πi/3} = −½ + (√3/2)i.
2. 2ω = −1 + √3 i, so a vertex is 1 + i − 1 + √3 i = (1 + √3)i, that is 0 + (1 + √3)i.
3. 2ω² = −1 − √3 i, so the other vertex is (1 − √3)i.

Show that cos(2π/5) + cos(4π/5) = −½.

1. The fifth roots of unity sum to 0: 1 + ω + ω² + ω³ + ω⁴ = 0.
2. ω⁴ = ω*, ω³ = (ω²)*, so the real parts give 1 + 2 cos(2π/5) + 2 cos(4π/5) = 0.
3. Hence cos(2π/5) + cos(4π/5) = −½.

ω is a complex cube root of unity. Simplify (1 + ω)(1 + ω²).

1. Expand: 1 + ω + ω² + ω³ = 0 + 1 = 1.

#### Common mistakes

- Rotating about the origin rather than about the polygon's centre.
- Forgetting ω³ = 1 when simplifying powers.

### Check questions

1. Write z = √3 − i in exponential form, and hence find z⁶. [3 marks]
2. Use de Moivre's theorem to show that cos 5θ = 16cos⁵θ − 20cos³θ + 5cos θ. [5 marks]
3. Show that sin⁴θ = (cos 4θ − 4 cos 2θ + 3)/8, and find ∫₀^{π/4} sin⁴θ dθ in exact form. [6 marks]
4. Solve z⁴ = 8 + 8√3 i, giving your answers in the form re^{iθ} with −π < θ ≤ π. [4 marks]
5. Prove by induction that (cos θ + i sin θ)ⁿ = cos nθ + i sin nθ for all positive integers n. [5 marks]
6. A square has centre 2 − i and one vertex at 5 + i. Find the other three vertices. [4 marks]
7. ω = e^{2πi/7}. Show that ω + ω² + ω³ + ω⁴ + ω⁵ + ω⁶ = −1, and deduce the value of cos(2π/7) + cos(4π/7) + cos(6π/7). [4 marks]
8. Show that Σ_{k=1}^{n} sin kθ = sin(nθ/2) sin((n + 1)θ/2)/sin(θ/2), for θ not a multiple of 2π. [6 marks]

### Mark scheme

1. z = 2e^{−iπ/6} (1). z⁶ = 64e^{−iπ} (1) = −64 (1).
2. Real part of (c + is)⁵ = c⁵ − 10c³s² + 5cs⁴ (2). Replace s² = 1 − c² (1). c⁵ − 10c³ + 10c⁵ + 5c(1 − 2c² + c⁴) (1) = 16c⁵ − 20c³ + 5c (1).
3. (z − 1/z)⁴ = (2i sin θ)⁴ = 16 sin⁴θ (1). = (z⁴ + z⁻⁴) − 4(z² + z⁻²) + 6 = 2cos 4θ − 8cos 2θ + 6 (2). Result (1). Integral [sin 4θ/4 − 2 sin 2θ + 3θ]/8 from 0 to π/4 (1) = (−2 + 3π/4)/8 = 3π/32 − 1/4 (1).
4. 8 + 8√3 i = 16e^{iπ/3} (1). z = 2e^{i(π/12 + kπ/2)} (1). Arguments π/12, 7π/12, −5π/12, −11π/12 (2).
5. n = 1 true (1). Assume for k (1). Multiply by cos θ + i sin θ (1); compound angle formulae give cos(k + 1)θ + i sin(k + 1)θ (1). Conclusion (1).
6. p − c = 3 + 2i (1). Multiply by i: −2 + 3i, giving vertex 2i (1). By −1: 2 − i − 3 − 2i = −1 − 3i (1). By −i: 2 − 3i, giving 4 − 4i (1).
7. ω⁷ = 1 and ω ≠ 1, so 1 + ω + ... + ω⁶ = 0 (1), giving the result (1). Real parts in conjugate pairs: 2[cos(2π/7) + cos(4π/7) + cos(6π/7)] = −1 (1), so the sum is −½ (1).
8. Imaginary part of Σ e^{ikθ} = e^{iθ}(e^{inθ} − 1)/(e^{iθ} − 1) (2). Half-angle factors: e^{i(n+1)θ/2} sin(nθ/2)/sin(θ/2) (2). Imaginary part gives the result (2).
