5. A particle on a light rod of length 1 m moves in a vertical circle. Find the least speed at the bottom for it to reach the top. [2 marks]

### Mark scheme

1. ω = 4π (1). T = 0.4 × 0.6 × (4π)² (1) = 37.9 N (1).
2. Radius of circle √(0.4² − 0.2²) = 0.3464 m (1). Reaction at angle with tan(angle to vertical) = 0.3464/0.2 (1). R cos α = mg, R sin α = mv²/r (1). v² = rg tan α = 0.3464 × 9.8 × 1.732 = 5.88 (1). v = 2.42 m s⁻¹ (1).
3. At angle a above horizontal, v² = 25 − 2g × 0.8(1 + sin a) (2). T = 0 gives v² = 0.8g sin a (1). 25 − 15.68 = 23.52 sin a (1), a = 23.3° (1).
4. Horizontal: R sin 15° + F cos 15° = 900 × 625/80 = 7031 (2). Vertical: R cos 15° − F sin 15° = 8820 (2). Solve: F = 7031 cos 15° − 8820 sin 15° = 4509 N (1), down the slope (1).
5. u² = 4gr = 39.2, u = 6.26 m s⁻¹ (just over) (2).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/further-maths/47-centres-of-mass.md -->

## Centres of mass

This file covers the centre of mass of systems of particles in one and two dimensions, using symmetry and standard results, composite bodies and laminas (including removing parts), centres of mass by integration for laminas and solids of revolution, and bodies suspended freely or resting on a plane.

### Overview

Moments are in A-level Maths ([../maths/29-moments.md](../maths/29-moments.md)). Integration for volumes is in [12-further-calculus-volumes-improper-integrals-and-mean-value.md](12-further-calculus-volumes-improper-integrals-and-mean-value.md). Equilibrium, toppling and sliding are in [48-rigid-bodies-forces-and-equilibrium.md](48-rigid-bodies-forces-and-equilibrium.md).

#### Board differences

- AQA ME1 to ME5; Edexcel FM2 2.2, 3.1; OCR 6.04a to 6.04d; MEI MG1 to MG10.
- Standard results (rods, triangles, arcs, sectors, hemispheres, cones) are in each board's formula book; use them unless asked to derive.

### ME1 Systems of particles

*Refs: AQA ME1 · Edexcel FM2 2.2 · OCR 6.04a, 6.04b · MEI MG1. Level: AS & A (MEI).*

#### What you need to know

```latex
\bar x = \frac{\sum m_ix_i}{\sum m_i},\qquad \bar y = \frac{\sum m_iy_i}{\sum m_i}
```

- In vector form, r̄ = Σmᵢrᵢ/Σmᵢ.

#### Worked example

Particles of mass 3, 2 and 5 kg are at (2, 1), (−1, 4) and (5, −2). Find the centre of mass.

1. x̄ = (6 − 2 + 25)/10 = 2.9. ȳ = (3 + 8 − 10)/10 = 0.1.

#### Common mistakes

- Dividing by the number of particles instead of the total mass.

### ME2 Composite bodies

*Refs: AQA ME2 · Edexcel FM2 2.2, 3.1 · OCR 6.04c · MEI MG2, MG3, MG4. Level: AS & A (MEI).*

#### What you need to know

- Use symmetry where possible.
- For a uniform lamina, mass is proportional to area. Treat each part as a particle at its own centre of mass.
- To remove a part, give it negative mass (area).
- Standard results: uniform triangle at the mean of the vertices; semicircular lamina 4r/(3π) from the diameter; solid hemisphere 3r/8; solid cone h/4 from the base.

#### Worked example

A uniform L-shaped lamina is made from a 6 × 2 rectangle (from (0, 0) to (6, 2)) and a 2 × 4 rectangle on top of its left end (from (0, 2) to (2, 6)). Find its centre of mass.

1. Parts: area 12 at (3, 1); area 8 at (1, 4).
2. x̄ = (36 + 8)/20 = 2.2. ȳ = (12 + 32)/20 = 2.2.

A circle of radius 1 centred at (4, 3) is cut from a uniform rectangular plate from (0, 0) to (8, 6). Find the centre of mass.

1. Plate area 48 at (4, 3); hole area π at (4, 3). The hole is at the centre, so the centre of mass stays at (4, 3).
2. If instead the hole is centred at (6, 3): x̄ = (48 × 4 − π × 6)/(48 − π) = 3.860.

#### Common mistakes

- Adding the removed part instead of subtracting it.

### ME3 Centres of mass by integration

*Refs: AQA ME3, ME4 · Edexcel FM2 2.2, 3.1 · OCR 6.04d · MEI MG6, MG7, MG8, MG9. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

For a uniform lamina under y = f(x) from x = a to b:

```latex
\bar x = \frac{\int_a^b xy\,dx}{\int_a^b y\,dx},\qquad \bar y = \frac{\int_a^b \tfrac12y^2\,dx}{\int_a^b y\,dx}
```

For a uniform solid of revolution about the x-axis:

```latex
\bar x = \frac{\int_a^b \pi xy^2\,dx}{\int_a^b \pi y^2\,dx}
```

#### Explained

Each vertical strip of width δx has area yδx and its own centre at (x, y/2). Summing moments gives the formulae. For solids, each disc has volume πy²δx at distance x.

#### Worked example

Find the centre of mass of the uniform lamina under y = x² from x = 0 to 2.

1. Area ∫₀² x² dx = 8/3.
2. x̄ = ∫₀² x³ dx ÷ 8/3 = 4 × 3/8 = 1.5.
3. ȳ = ½∫₀² x⁴ dx ÷ 8/3 = (16/5)(3/8) = 1.2.

The region under y = √x from x = 0 to 4 is rotated about the x-axis. Find the centre of mass of the solid.

1. Volume π∫₀⁴ x dx = 8π.
2. x̄ = π∫₀⁴ x² dx/8π = (64/3)/8 = 8/3.

Prove that a uniform solid hemisphere of radius r has its centre of mass 3r/8 from the plane face.

1. Rotate y² = r² − x², 0 ≤ x ≤ r. Volume π∫(r² − x²) dx = 2πr³/3.
2. π∫x(r² − x²) dx = π(r⁴/2 − r⁴/4) = πr⁴/4.
3. x̄ = (πr⁴/4)/(2πr³/3) = 3r/8.

#### Common mistakes

- Using y instead of y/2 for the lamina's ȳ.

### ME5 Suspension and resting on a plane

*Refs: AQA ME5 · OCR 6.04e · MEI MG5, MG10. Level: AS & A (MEI).*

#### What you need to know

- A body suspended freely from a point hangs with its centre of mass vertically below that point.
- A body on an inclined plane is on the point of toppling when the centre of mass is vertically above the lowest edge of contact.

#### Worked example

The L-shaped lamina above is suspended from the origin. Find the angle between the side along the x-axis and the vertical.

1. The line from (0, 0) to G(2.2, 2.2) is vertical.
2. It makes 45° with the x-axis, so the x-axis side is at 45° to the vertical.

A uniform cuboid 0.4 m wide and 1.2 m tall stands on a rough slope (friction prevents sliding). Find the angle at which it topples.

1. Topples when tan α = (half-width)/(height of G) = 0.2/0.6, α = 18.4°.

### Check questions

1. Masses of 1, 2, 3 and 4 kg are at the corners (0, 0), (4, 0), (4, 3), (0, 3) of a light rectangle. Find the centre of mass. [3 marks]
2. A uniform lamina is a square of side 6 with a square of side 2 removed from one corner. Find the distance of the centre of mass from each of the two full sides that meet at the corner opposite the removed square. [5 marks]
3. Find the centre of mass of the uniform lamina bounded by y = 4 − x², the x-axis and the y-axis (x ≥ 0). [6 marks]
4. Find the centre of mass of the uniform solid formed by rotating y = x, 0 ≤ x ≤ 3, about the x-axis (a cone). [4 marks]
5. State the distance of the centre of mass of a uniform solid hemisphere of radius 0.3 m from the centre of its plane face. [2 marks]

### Mark scheme

1. Total 10 (1). x̄ = (8 + 12)/10 = 2 (1). ȳ = (9 + 12)/10 = 2.1 (1).
2. Take the full square with corner at origin (area 36, centre (3, 3)) and remove the 2 × 2 square at the far corner (area 4, centre (5, 5)) (2). x̄ = (108 − 20)/32 = 2.75 (2). By symmetry ȳ = 2.75: distance 2.75 from each side through the origin (1).
3. Area ∫₀² (4 − x²) dx = 16/3 (1). ∫x(4 − x²) dx = 4 (1), x̄ = 3/4 (1). ½∫(4 − x²)² dx = ½(32 − 64/3 + 32/5) = 128/15 (2), ȳ = 1.6 (1).
4. Volume π∫x² dx = 9π (1). π∫x³ dx = 81π/4 (1). x̄ = 9/4 (from the vertex), so 3/4 from the base, which is h/4 (2).
5. 3r/8 = 0.1125 m (2).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/further-maths/48-rigid-bodies-forces-and-equilibrium.md -->

## Forces, moments and equilibrium of rigid bodies

This file covers forces on particles (contact forces, friction, resolving, the triangle of forces) as MEI revisits them, moments and couples, the conditions for a rigid body to be in equilibrium, problems with ladders, hinged rods and strings, and deciding whether a body will slide or topple.

### Overview

Much of the particle work (marked † in the MEI specification) is shared with A-level Maths: see [../maths/27-forces-and-newtons-laws.md](../maths/27-forces-and-newtons-laws.md), [../maths/28-friction.md](../maths/28-friction.md) and [../maths/29-moments.md](../maths/29-moments.md). Centres of mass are in [47-centres-of-mass.md](47-centres-of-mass.md). Take g = 9.8 m s⁻².

#### Board differences

- AQA ME5, ME6 (sliding, toppling, suspension; forces on rigid bodies, moments and couples).
- Edexcel FM2 2.1 (moments), 3.2 (equilibrium of rigid bodies), 3.3 (toppling and sliding).
- OCR 6.04e (equilibrium of rigid bodies, including toppling).
- MEI Md1 to Md16 (Mechanics Major and Minor): Md1 to Md10 are particle forces and friction, including the result that a body on a rough plane is about to slip when tan α = μ (Md4) and closed polygons of forces (Md9); Md11 to Md16 are rigid bodies.

### Forces on particles

*Refs: MEI Md1, Md2, Md3, Md4, Md5, Md6, Md7, Md8, Md9, Md10. Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- The contact force between rough surfaces has a normal component R and a frictional component F ≤ μR, with F = μR when slipping or about to slip.
- Resolve in two perpendicular directions; in equilibrium each resolved sum is zero. Three forces in equilibrium form a closed triangle (use the sine or cosine rule).
- (Md4) A body on a rough plane inclined at α, with no other forces, is about to slip when tan α = μ.

#### Worked example

Show that a block on a rough plane is on the point of sliding when tan α = μ.

1. Perpendicular: R = mg cos α. Along: F = mg sin α.
2. Limiting: mg sin α = μmg cos α, so tan α = μ.

#### Common mistakes

- Assuming F = μR when the body is not about to slip.

### ME6 Moments, couples and rigid-body equilibrium

*Refs: AQA ME6 · Edexcel FM2 2.1, 3.2 · OCR 6.04e · MEI Md11, Md12, Md13, Md14, Md15. Level: AS & A (MEI); A-level (others).*

#### What you need to know

- Moment of F about a point = F × perpendicular distance from the point to the line of action.
- A couple is a pair of equal, opposite, parallel forces not in line. It has a turning effect (moment Fd, the same about every point) but no resultant force.
- A rigid body is in equilibrium if the resultant force is zero and the total moment about any point is zero.
- Take moments about a point where unknown forces act, to remove them from the equation.

#### Worked example

A uniform ladder of length 4 m and mass 20 kg rests against a smooth wall at 60° to rough horizontal ground. Find the least μ for equilibrium.

1. Forces: weight 20g at the midpoint, wall reaction S (horizontal), ground R and friction F.
2. Vertical: R = 20g. Horizontal: F = S.
3. Moments about the foot: S × 4 sin 60° = 20g × 2 cos 60°, so S = 10g cot 60° = 56.6 N.
4. F ≤ μR: μ ≥ 56.6/196 = 0.289.

A uniform horizontal rod of length 2 m and weight 50 N is hinged to a wall at A and held by a string from its end B to a point on the wall above A, at 30° to the rod. Find the tension and the hinge force.

1. Moments about A: T sin 30° × 2 = 50 × 1, T = 50 N.
2. Horizontal: H = T cos 30° = 43.3 N. Vertical: V = 50 − 25 = 25 N.
3. Hinge force √(43.3² + 25²) = 50 N, at 30° above the horizontal.

#### Common mistakes

- Using the length along the rod instead of the perpendicular distance.
- Forgetting the hinge reaction can act in any direction (two components).

### ME5 Sliding or toppling

*Refs: AQA ME5 · Edexcel FM2 3.3 · MEI Md16. Level: AS & A (MEI); A-level (others).*

#### What you need to know

- On the point of toppling, the normal reaction acts at the edge about which the body would tip.
- Compare the force (or angle) needed to cause sliding with that needed for toppling; the smaller one happens first.

#### Worked example

A uniform cube of side 0.4 m and weight 100 N on rough ground (μ = 0.6) is pushed by a horizontal force P at height 0.3 m. Does it slide or topple first as P increases?

1. Sliding: P = μR = 60 N.
2. Toppling about the front edge: P × 0.3 = 100 × 0.2, P = 66.7 N.
3. 60 < 66.7: it slides first.

#### Common mistakes

- Taking moments about the centre instead of the toppling edge.

### Check questions

1. A uniform beam AB of length 6 m and weight 200 N rests on supports at C and D, 1 m from each end. A 300 N weight is placed 0.5 m from A. Find the support forces. [4 marks]
2. A uniform ladder of weight W and length 2a rests against a smooth wall with its foot on rough ground (μ = 0.4). Find the smallest angle with the ground for equilibrium. [5 marks]
3. A couple and a force act on a lamina. Explain why the moment of a couple is the same about every point. [2 marks]
4. A uniform cuboid 0.5 m wide and 1 m tall, weight 80 N, is on rough ground (μ = 0.3). A horizontal force P acts at the top. Find whether it slides or topples first. [4 marks]

### Mark scheme

1. Supports at 1 m and 5 m from A. Moments about C: D × 4 = 200 × 2 + 300 × (−0.5) (2), D = 62.5 N (1). C = 500 − 62.5 = 437.5 N (1).
2. R = W, F = S (1). Moments about the foot: S × 2a sin θ = Wa cos θ (2), S = (W/2) cot θ (1). ½ cot θ ≤ 0.4 gives tan θ ≥ 1.25, θ ≥ 51.3° (1).
3. Forces F at distance d apart: about any point, moments Fx and F(d − x) or F(x + d) − Fx combine to Fd (2).
4. Slide: P = 0.3 × 80 = 24 N (1). Topple about the far bottom edge: P × 1 = 80 × 0.25, P = 20 N (2). 20 < 24: it topples first (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/further-maths/30-discrete-random-variables.md -->

## Discrete random variables

This file covers discrete random variables given by tables or formulae, probabilities, expectation, variance, mode and median, the expectation of functions of a random variable, linear coding, the discrete uniform distribution (with proofs of its mean and variance), the mean and variance of the binomial distribution, and probabilities found by counting arrangements.

### Overview

Probability and the binomial distribution are in A-level Maths ([../maths/21-probability.md](../maths/21-probability.md), [../maths/22-statistical-distributions.md](../maths/22-statistical-distributions.md)). Sums of independent random variables are in [36-linear-combinations-of-random-variables.md](36-linear-combinations-of-random-variables.md); continuous variables in [35-continuous-random-variables.md](35-continuous-random-variables.md).

#### Board differences

- AQA SA1 to SA7; Edexcel FS1 1.1; OCR 5.01a, 5.01b, 5.02a to 5.02e; MEI SR1 to SR10.
- AQA SA7 requires the proofs of the mean and variance of the discrete uniform distribution. AQA SA3 includes mode and median.
- OCR 5.01 includes probability by counting (permutations and combinations); the counting methods are in [59-combinatorics.md](59-combinatorics.md). OCR 5.02d and MEI SR9 ask for the binomial mean np and variance np(1 − p).

### SA1 Probability distributions

*Refs: AQA SA1, SA2 · Edexcel FS1 1.1 · OCR 5.01a, 5.01b, 5.02a · MEI SR1. Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- A discrete random variable X takes values x₁, x₂, ... with probabilities pᵢ = P(X = xᵢ), each between 0 and 1, with Σpᵢ = 1.
- The distribution may be given as a table or a formula (for example P(X = x) = kx for x = 1, 2, 3, 4).
- The cumulative distribution function F(x) = P(X ≤ x).

#### Worked example

P(X = x) = kx for x = 1, 2, 3, 4. Find k and P(X ≥ 3).

1. k(1 + 2 + 3 + 4) = 1, k = 1/10.
2. P(X ≥ 3) = 0.3 + 0.4 = 0.7.

(OCR) Three letters are chosen at random from the word ORANGE without replacement. X is the number of vowels chosen. Find the distribution of X.

1. Vowels O, A, E (3); consonants R, N, G (3). C(6, 3) = 20 selections.
2. P(X = 0) = 1/20, P(X = 1) = 3 × 3/20 = 9/20, P(X = 2) = 9/20, P(X = 3) = 1/20.

#### Common mistakes

- Forgetting to check that probabilities sum to 1.

### SA3 Expectation and variance

*Refs: AQA SA3, SA4, SA5 · Edexcel FS1 1.1 · OCR 5.02b, 5.02c · MEI SR2, SR3, SR4, SR5, SR6. Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

```latex
E(X) = \mu = \sum x_ip_i,\qquad E(X^2) = \sum x_i^2p_i,\qquad \mathrm{Var}(X) = E(X^2) - [E(X)]^2
```

- E(g(X)) = Σg(xᵢ)pᵢ.
- E(aX + b) = aE(X) + b; Var(aX + b) = a²Var(X).
- Standard deviation = √Var(X). Mode: the most likely value. Median m: the smallest value with F(m) ≥ ½.

#### Explained

E(X) is the long-run average of many observations. Variance measures spread. Adding b shifts every value, which changes the mean but not the spread; multiplying by a scales the spread by |a|, so the variance by a².

#### Worked example

| x | 0 | 1 | 2 | 3 |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| P(X = x) | 0.1 | 0.3 | 0.4 | 0.2 |

Find E(X), Var(X), E(1/(X + 1)) and Var(3X − 2).

1. E(X) = 0 + 0.3 + 0.8 + 0.6 = 1.7.
2. E(X²) = 0 + 0.3 + 1.6 + 1.8 = 3.7. Var(X) = 3.7 − 2.89 = 0.81.
3. E(1/(X + 1)) = 0.1 + 0.3/2 + 0.4/3 + 0.2/4 = 13/30.
4. Var(3X − 2) = 9 × 0.81 = 7.29.
5. Mode 2. F(1) = 0.4, F(2) = 0.8, so the median is 2.

#### Common mistakes

- Var(3X − 2) = 3Var(X) − 2.
- E(1/X) = 1/E(X).

### SA6 The discrete uniform distribution

*Refs: AQA SA6, SA7 · OCR 5.02e · MEI SR7, SR8, SR9. Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- X is uniform on {1, 2, ..., n} if P(X = r) = 1/n for each r. Use it when every outcome is equally likely (a fair die, a random digit).

```latex
E(X) = \frac{n+1}{2},\qquad \mathrm{Var}(X) = \frac{n^2-1}{12}
```

#### Explained

Proof (AQA SA7):

```latex
E(X) = \frac1n\sum_{r=1}^n r = \frac1n\cdot\frac{n(n+1)}{2} = \frac{n+1}{2}
```

```latex
E(X^2) = \frac1n\cdot\frac{n(n+1)(2n+1)}{6} = \frac{(n+1)(2n+1)}{6}
```

```latex
\mathrm{Var}(X) = \frac{(n+1)(2n+1)}{6} - \frac{(n+1)^2}{4} = \frac{(n+1)(4n+2-3n-3)}{12} = \frac{n^2-1}{12}
```

#### Worked example

A spinner shows 1 to 10 equally likely. Find the mean and variance of the score.

1. Mean 11/2 = 5.5. Variance 99/12 = 8.25.

Y is uniform on {2, 4, 6, ..., 20}. Find E(Y) and Var(Y).

1. Y = 2X with X uniform on 1 to 10. E(Y) = 11, Var(Y) = 4 × 8.25 = 33.

#### Common mistakes

- Using the formula for values that do not start at 1 without coding.

### Mean and variance of the binomial distribution

*Refs: OCR 5.02d · MEI SR10. Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- If X ~ B(n, p), E(X) = np and Var(X) = np(1 − p).

#### Worked example

X ~ B(20, 0.3). Find E(X), Var(X) and P(X > E(X)).

1. E(X) = 6, Var(X) = 4.2.
2. P(X > 6) = 1 − P(X ≤ 6) = 1 − 0.6080 = 0.3920.

### Check questions

1. P(X = x) = c(x² + 1) for x = 0, 1, 2, 3. Find c, E(X) and Var(X). [6 marks]
2. X has E(X) = 4 and Var(X) = 2. Find E(5 − 2X), Var(5 − 2X) and E(X²). [4 marks]
3. A fair 8-sided die is rolled. Find the mean and standard deviation of the score, and the probability that the score is within one standard deviation of the mean. [5 marks]
4. Prove that the variance of the discrete uniform distribution on {1, ..., n} is (n² − 1)/12. [4 marks]
5. Two cards are drawn without replacement from five cards numbered 1 to 5. X is the larger number. Find the distribution of X, its mode, median and mean. [6 marks]

### Mark scheme

1. c(1 + 2 + 5 + 10) = 1, c = 1/18 (1). Probabilities 1/18, 2/18, 5/18, 10/18 (1). E(X) = (0 + 2 + 10 + 30)/18 = 7/3 (1). E(X²) = (0 + 2 + 20 + 90)/18 = 56/9 (1). Var = 56/9 − 49/9 = 7/9 (2).
2. 5 − 8 = −3 (1). 4 × 2 = 8 (1). E(X²) = 2 + 16 = 18 (2).
3. Mean 4.5 (1). Variance 63/12 = 5.25, s.d. 2.291 (2). Within 2.209 to 6.791: scores 3, 4, 5, 6 (1), probability ½ (1).
4. E(X) and E(X²) from standard sums (2). Subtract and simplify (2).
5. 10 equally likely pairs (1). P(X = 2) = 1/10, P(X = 3) = 2/10, P(X = 4) = 3/10, P(X = 5) = 4/10 (2). Mode 5 (1). F(4) = 0.6 ≥ ½, F(3) = 0.3, so median 4 (1). Mean (2 + 6 + 12 + 20)/10 = 4 (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/further-maths/31-poisson-geometric-and-negative-binomial-distributions.md -->

## Poisson, geometric and negative binomial distributions

This file covers the Poisson distribution (conditions, probabilities, mean and variance, sums of independent Poissons, approximating the binomial), the geometric distribution and (Edexcel) the negative binomial distribution, with their means and variances.

### Overview

The binomial distribution is in A-level Maths ([../maths/22-statistical-distributions.md](../maths/22-statistical-distributions.md)); expectation and variance in [30-discrete-random-variables.md](30-discrete-random-variables.md). Tests using these distributions are in [32-hypothesis-tests-errors-and-power.md](32-hypothesis-tests-errors-and-power.md). The exponential distribution of the gaps between Poisson events is in [35-continuous-random-variables.md](35-continuous-random-variables.md).

#### Board differences

- Poisson: AQA SB1 to SB4; Edexcel FS1 2.1 to 2.3; OCR 5.02i to 5.02n; MEI SR11 to SR15.
- Geometric: Edexcel FS1 3.1, 3.2; OCR 5.02f to 5.02h; MEI SR16 to SR18. AQA does not include it.
- Negative binomial: Edexcel FS1 3.1, 3.3 only.
- The Poisson approximation to the binomial is named by Edexcel FS1 2.3 and MEI SR12.

### SB1 The Poisson distribution

*Refs: AQA SB1, SB2, SB3 · Edexcel FS1 2.1, 2.2 · OCR 5.02i, 5.02j, 5.02k, 5.02l, 5.02m · MEI SR11, SR13, SR14. Level: AS & A (OCR, MEI).*

#### What you need to know

```latex
X\sim\mathrm{Po}(\lambda):\quad P(X=x) = \frac{e^{-\lambda}\lambda^x}{x!},\quad x = 0, 1, 2, \dots
```

- E(X) = Var(X) = λ.
- Conditions: events occur singly, independently, at random, and at a constant average rate. The rate scales with the interval: 3 per hour means 1.5 per half hour.
- A mean close to the variance in data supports a Poisson model.

#### Explained

The Poisson counts how many times something happens in a fixed interval of time or space: calls to a helpline in an hour, flaws in a metre of cable. Use the formula for single values and the calculator's cumulative function for ranges. "At least 5" is 1 − P(X ≤ 4).

#### Worked example

Emails arrive at a rate of 2.5 per hour. Find the probability of exactly 3 in an hour, at most 3, and more than 4.

1. P(X = 3) = e^{−2.5} × 2.5³/6 = 0.2138.
2. P(X ≤ 3) = 0.7576.
3. P(X > 4) = 1 − P(X ≤ 4) = 0.1088.

Cars pass a point at 6 per minute. Find P(3 ≤ X ≤ 5) in one minute.

1. P(X ≤ 5) − P(X ≤ 2) = 0.4457 − 0.0620 = 0.3837.

Data: mean 1.95, variance 2.04. Comment on a Poisson model.

1. Mean and variance are close, which supports a Poisson model (as long as the conditions are plausible).

#### Common mistakes

- Not rescaling λ to the interval in the question.
- P(X > 4) = 1 − P(X ≤ 3).

### SB4 Sums of independent Poisson variables

*Refs: AQA SB4 · Edexcel FS1 2.2 · OCR 5.02n · MEI SR15. Level: AS & A (OCR, MEI).*

#### What you need to know

- If X ~ Po(λ) and Y ~ Po(μ) are independent, X + Y ~ Po(λ + μ).

#### Worked example

Faults in type A cable occur at 1.2 per 100 m and in type B at 2.1 per 100 m, independently. Find the probability of exactly 3 faults in total in 100 m of each.

1. Total ~ Po(3.3). P(T = 3) = e^{−3.3} × 3.3³/6 = 0.2209.

Find the probability of 2 faults in A and 1 in B.

1. 0.2169 × 0.2572 = 0.0558 (multiply, as they are independent).

#### Common mistakes

- Adding λs of variables that are not independent.

### The Poisson approximation to the binomial

*Refs: Edexcel FS1 2.3 · MEI SR12. Level: A-level (Edexcel), AS & A (MEI).*

#### What you need to know

- If n is large and p is small, B(n, p) ≈ Po(np).

#### Worked example

X ~ B(200, 0.01). Estimate P(X ≤ 3).

1. np = 2. Po(2): P(X ≤ 3) = 0.8571. (Exact binomial 0.8580.)

### The geometric distribution

*Refs: Edexcel FS1 3.1, 3.2 · OCR 5.02f, 5.02g, 5.02h · MEI SR16, SR17, SR18. Level: AS & A (OCR, MEI).*

#### What you need to know

- X ~ Geo(p) is the number of trials up to and including the first success, with independent trials and constant p.

```latex
P(X = x) = (1-p)^{x-1}p,\qquad P(X > x) = (1-p)^x,\qquad E(X) = \frac1p,\qquad \mathrm{Var}(X) = \frac{1-p}{p^2}
```

#### Explained

P(X > x) = (1 − p)ˣ because the first x trials must all fail. That gives P(X ≤ x) = 1 − (1 − p)ˣ, which avoids summing many terms.

#### Worked example

A player scores a penalty with probability 0.8. X is the number of the penalty on which they first miss. Find P(X = 4), P(X > 5) and E(X).

1. Here "success" is a miss, p = 0.2.
2. P(X = 4) = 0.8³ × 0.2 = 0.1024. P(X > 5) = 0.8⁵ = 0.3277. E(X) = 5.

#### Common mistakes

- Using (1 − p)ˣp instead of (1 − p)^{x−1}p.

### The negative binomial distribution

*Refs: Edexcel FS1 3.1, 3.3. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- X is the number of trials needed to get r successes:

```latex
P(X = x) = \binom{x-1}{r-1}p^r(1-p)^{x-r},\quad x = r, r+1, \dots
```

- E(X) = r/p, Var(X) = r(1 − p)/p².

#### Explained

The last trial must be the rth success; the other r − 1 successes can be anywhere in the first x − 1 trials. With r = 1 it is the geometric distribution. X is a sum of r independent geometric variables, which explains the mean and variance.

#### Worked example

p = 0.4. Find the probability that the third success comes on the seventh trial, and the mean and variance of the number of trials needed for 3 successes.

1. C(6, 2) × 0.4³ × 0.6⁴ = 15 × 0.064 × 0.1296 = 0.1244.
2. E(X) = 3/0.4 = 7.5. Var(X) = 3 × 0.6/0.16 = 11.25.

#### Common mistakes

- Using C(x, r) instead of C(x − 1, r − 1).

### Check questions

1. A shop sells on average 3.5 kettles a week. (a) State two conditions for a Poisson model. (b) Find the probability of selling exactly 5 in a week, and at least 8 in two weeks. [6 marks]
2. X ~ Po(λ) and P(X = 2) = P(X = 3). Find λ and P(X ≤ 2). [4 marks]
3. A die is rolled until a six appears. Find the probability that more than 6 rolls are needed, and the variance of the number of rolls. [4 marks]
4. (Edexcel) A coin with P(head) = 0.3 is tossed until the fourth head. Find the probability that exactly 10 tosses are needed, and the expected number of tosses. [4 marks]
5. X ~ Po(1.5) and Y ~ Po(2.5) independently. Find P(X + Y = 4) and P(X = 1 | X + Y = 4). [5 marks]

### Mark scheme

1. (a) Sales occur independently, singly, at a constant average rate (2). (b) Po(3.5): P(X = 5) = 0.1322 (2). Po(7): P(Y ≥ 8) = 1 − 0.5987 = 0.4013 (2).
2. e^{−λ}λ²/2 = e^{−λ}λ³/6 (1), λ = 3 (1). P(X ≤ 2) = e^{−3}(1 + 3 + 4.5) = 0.4232 (2).
3. P(X > 6) = (5/6)⁶ = 0.3349 (2). Var = (5/6)/(1/36) = 30 (2).
4. C(9, 3) × 0.3⁴ × 0.7⁶ = 84 × 0.0081 × 0.117649 (1) = 0.0800 (1). E = 4/0.3 = 13.3 (2).
5. X + Y ~ Po(4): P = e^{−4} × 4⁴/24 = 0.1954 (2). P(X = 1, Y = 3)/P(X + Y = 4) (1) = (0.3347 × 0.2138)/0.1954 = 0.3662 (2). (Exactly C(4, 1)(0.375)(0.625)³ = 0.3662.)

<!-- source: notes/a-level/further-maths/32-hypothesis-tests-errors-and-power.md -->

## Hypothesis tests: Poisson and geometric tests, Type I and II errors, power

This file covers hypothesis tests for the mean of a Poisson distribution and for the parameter of a geometric distribution, extended binomial tests, critical regions and actual significance levels, Type I and Type II errors, and the power of a test.

### Overview

The language of hypothesis testing and the binomial test are in A-level Maths ([../maths/23-hypothesis-testing.md](../maths/23-hypothesis-testing.md)). The distributions are in [31-poisson-geometric-and-negative-binomial-distributions.md](31-poisson-geometric-and-negative-binomial-distributions.md). Tests using normal and t distributions are in [38-confidence-intervals-and-tests-for-means.md](38-confidence-intervals-and-tests-for-means.md).

#### Board differences

- AQA SB5 (Poisson mean test), SC1 (Type I and Type II errors for Poisson tests), SC2 (power for tests based on normal, binomial or Poisson distributions).
- Edexcel FS1 4.1 (tests for binomial and Poisson parameters, including critical regions), 4.2 (tests for a geometric parameter), 8.1 (Type I and Type II errors, size and power, power functions).
- OCR and MEI do not list Type II errors or power; their Poisson-based inference is through chi-squared and normal tests.

### SB5 Testing a Poisson mean

*Refs: AQA SB5 · Edexcel FS1 4.1. Level: A-level (option).*

#### What you need to know

- H₀: λ = λ₀ against H₁: λ > λ₀, λ < λ₀ or λ ≠ λ₀, using a single observation (or a total over several intervals, which is Poisson with the scaled mean).
- Compare P(X ≥ x) (or P(X ≤ x)) under H₀ with the significance level, or find the critical region.
- The actual significance level is the probability of the critical region under H₀.
- For a two-tailed test at α, put at most α/2 in each tail.

#### Explained

Because the distribution is discrete, the actual significance level is usually less than the stated one. Always write the conclusion in context, and never say H₀ is "proved".

#### Worked example

A call centre normally receives 4 complaints a day. After a change, 8 complaints arrive in one day. Test at the 5% level whether the rate has increased.

1. H₀: λ = 4, H₁: λ > 4. X ~ Po(4) under H₀.
2. P(X ≥ 8) = 1 − P(X ≤ 7) = 0.0511 > 0.05.
3. Not significant: insufficient evidence that complaints have increased.
4. Critical region: P(X ≥ 9) = 0.0214, so X ≥ 9; actual significance level 2.14%.

Find the critical region for a two-tailed test of H₀: λ = 10 at the 5% level.

1. Lower tail: P(X ≤ 3) = 0.0103, P(X ≤ 4) = 0.0293 > 0.025, so X ≤ 3.
2. Upper tail: P(X ≥ 17) = 0.0270 > 0.025, P(X ≥ 18) = 0.0143, so X ≥ 18.
3. Critical region X ≤ 3 or X ≥ 18; actual significance level 0.0246.

Over 2 weeks a shop expects 2.5 returns per week. It records 3 returns in the 2 weeks. Test at 5% whether the rate has fallen.

1. Total ~ Po(5) under H₀. P(X ≤ 3) = 0.2650 > 0.05. Not significant.

#### Common mistakes

- Using P(X = 8) instead of P(X ≥ 8).
- Forgetting to scale λ for several intervals.

### Testing a geometric parameter

*Refs: Edexcel FS1 4.2. Level: A-level (option).*

#### What you need to know

- Use one observation of X ~ Geo(p). A large X suggests p is smaller than claimed; a small X suggests p is larger.
- P(X ≥ x) = (1 − p)^{x−1}; P(X ≤ x) = 1 − (1 − p)ˣ.

#### Worked example

A game claims a 1 in 5 chance of winning on each play. A player's first win comes on the 14th play. Test at 5% whether the claim overstates p.

1. H₀: p = 0.2, H₁: p < 0.2.
2. P(X ≥ 14) = 0.8¹³ = 0.0550 > 0.05. Not significant at 5%.

#### Common mistakes

- Using 0.8¹⁴ for P(X ≥ 14).

### SC1 Type I and Type II errors

*Refs: AQA SC1 · Edexcel FS1 8.1. Level: A-level (option).*

#### What you need to know

- Type I error: rejecting H₀ when it is true. P(Type I error) = actual significance level (the size of the test).
- Type II error: accepting H₀ when it is false. Its probability depends on the true parameter value.
- Reducing one error type (at fixed sample size) increases the other.

#### Worked example

For the complaints test (critical region X ≥ 9), find P(Type I error), and P(Type II error) if the true rate is 7.

1. P(Type I) = P(X ≥ 9 | λ = 4) = 0.0214.
2. P(Type II) = P(X ≤ 8 | λ = 7) = 0.7291.

#### Common mistakes

- Giving the stated level (5%) as P(Type I error) for a discrete test.

### SC2 The power of a test

*Refs: AQA SC2 · Edexcel FS1 8.1. Level: A-level (option).*

#### What you need to know

- Power = P(reject H₀ | H₀ false) = 1 − P(Type II error).
- A power function gives the power for each possible true value of the parameter. A better test has higher power for the same size.
- For a normal mean test with X̄ ~ N(μ, σ²/n), find the critical value under H₀, then the probability of the critical region under the alternative μ.

#### Worked example

Power of the complaints test when λ = 7.

1. 1 − 0.7291 = 0.2709.

X ~ B(20, p). H₀: p = 0.3, H₁: p < 0.3, 5% level. Find the critical region, the size, and the power when p = 0.1.

1. P(X ≤ 2) = 0.0355, P(X ≤ 3) = 0.1071. Critical region X ≤ 2, size 0.0355.
2. Power = P(X ≤ 2 | p = 0.1) = 0.6769.

(AQA) X ~ N(μ, 16). A sample of 16 is used to test H₀: μ = 50 against H₁: μ > 50 at 5%. Find the power when μ = 52.

1. X̄ ~ N(50, 1) under H₀. Critical value 50 + 1.645 = 51.645.
2. Power = P(X̄ > 51.645 | μ = 52) = P(Z > −0.355) = 0.639.

#### Common mistakes

- Calculating the power under H₀ instead of under the alternative.

### Check questions

1. Accidents at a junction average 1.5 per month. After new lights, there are 2 accidents in 6 months. Test at the 5% level whether the rate has fallen. [5 marks]
2. X ~ Po(λ). Find the critical region for testing H₀: λ = 6 against H₁: λ < 6 at the 5% level, and the actual significance level. [3 marks]
3. For the test in question 2, find the probability of a Type II error when λ = 2, and the power. [3 marks]
4. (Edexcel) A machine produces faulty items with probability 0.02. To test whether p has increased, items are inspected until the first faulty one; X is the number inspected. Find the critical region at the 5% level and its actual significance level. [3 marks]
5. X ~ N(μ, 25), samples of 25. H₀: μ = 100, H₁: μ ≠ 100, 5% level. Find the acceptance region for x̄ and the probability of a Type II error when μ = 102. [5 marks]

### Mark scheme

1. H₀: λ = 9, H₁: λ < 9 (6-month total) (1). P(X ≤ 2 | Po(9)) = 0.0062 (2). < 0.05, reject H₀ (1). Evidence the accident rate has fallen (1).
2. P(X ≤ 1) = 0.0174, P(X ≤ 2) = 0.0620 (1). Critical region X ≤ 1 (1). Size 0.0174 (1).
3. P(Type II) = P(X ≥ 2 | λ = 2) = 1 − 0.4060 = 0.5940 (2). Power 0.4060 (1).
4. Small X supports H₁: P(X ≤ x) = 1 − 0.98ˣ ≤ 0.05 (1). 0.98ˣ ≥ 0.95 gives x ≤ 2.54, so the critical region is X ≤ 2 (1). Actual level 1 − 0.98² = 0.0396 (1).
5. X̄ ~ N(100, 1) (1). Accept 98.04 < x̄ < 101.96 (2). P(98.04 < X̄ < 101.96 | μ = 102) = P(−3.96 < Z < −0.04) (1) = 0.484 (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/further-maths/35-continuous-random-variables.md -->

## Continuous random variables, rectangular and exponential distributions

This file covers probability density functions, probabilities as areas, cumulative distribution functions, the median, quartiles and mode, the mean and variance (and E(g(X))), linear functions of a continuous variable, the distribution of a function of X, the continuous uniform (rectangular) distribution and the exponential distribution.

### Overview

Integration is from A-level Maths ([../maths/14-integration-basics-and-areas.md](../maths/14-integration-basics-and-areas.md)). Discrete variables are in [30-discrete-random-variables.md](30-discrete-random-variables.md); sums of variables in [36-linear-combinations-of-random-variables.md](36-linear-combinations-of-random-variables.md).

#### Board differences

- AQA SD1 to SD7 (including the rectangular distribution) and SF1 to SF4 (the exponential distribution, with proofs of its mean and variance and its link to the Poisson). Only AQA includes the exponential.
- Edexcel FS2 2.1 to 2.4 (including the continuous uniform distribution with derivations of its mean, variance and cdf).
- OCR 5.03a to 5.03g; 5.03g is finding the cdf of a function of X (such as Y = X²).
- MEI SR19 to SR27.

### SD1 Probability density functions

*Refs: AQA SD1, SD2 · Edexcel FS2 2.1, 2.2 · OCR 5.03a, 5.03b · MEI SR19, SR20, SR21. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- A continuous random variable X has a pdf f(x) ≥ 0 with ∫f(x) dx = 1 over all x.
- P(a < X < b) = ∫ₐᵇ f(x) dx. P(X = a) = 0, so < and ≤ give the same probability.
- A pdf can be defined piecewise.

#### Explained

Probability is area under the pdf, not the height. f(x) can be greater than 1, as long as the total area is 1.

#### Worked example

f(x) = kx² for 0 ≤ x ≤ 2, and 0 otherwise. Find k and P(X > 1).

1. k[x³/3]₀² = 8k/3 = 1, k = 3/8.
2. P(X > 1) = (3/8)[x³/3]₁² = (8 − 1)/8 = 7/8.

#### Common mistakes

- Reading f(a) as P(X = a).

### SD6 Cumulative distribution functions, median and quartiles

*Refs: AQA SD3, SD6 · Edexcel FS2 2.2 · OCR 5.03e, 5.03f, 5.03g · MEI SR23, SR24, SR25, SR26. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

```latex
F(x) = P(X \le x) = \int_{-\infty}^{x} f(t)\,dt,\qquad f(x) = F'(x)
```

- F is 0 below the range and 1 above it, and never decreases.
- Median m: F(m) = 0.5. Quartiles: F(q₁) = 0.25, F(q₃) = 0.75. Percentiles similarly.
- Mode: the value of x where f(x) is greatest (use calculus or a sketch).
- (OCR 5.03g) For Y = g(X) with g increasing, G(y) = P(Y ≤ y) = P(X ≤ g⁻¹(y)) = F(g⁻¹(y)). Differentiate for the pdf of Y.

#### Explained

For a piecewise pdf, F for each later piece must include the full area of earlier pieces, so that F is continuous.

#### Worked example

For f(x) = (3/8)x² on [0, 2], find F(x), the median and the mode.

1. F(x) = x³/8 for 0 ≤ x ≤ 2 (0 below, 1 above).
2. m³/8 = ½, m = ∛4 = 1.587.
3. f is increasing, so the mode is 2.

A piecewise pdf: f(x) = x for 0 ≤ x ≤ 1, f(x) = ½ for 1 < x ≤ 2. Find F(x) and the median.

1. Check area: ½ + ½ = 1 ✓.
2. 0 ≤ x ≤ 1: F = x²/2. 1 < x ≤ 2: F = ½ + (x − 1)/2 = x/2.
3. F(1) = ½, so the median is 1.

(OCR) For f(x) = (3/8)x² on [0, 2], find the pdf of Y = X².

1. G(y) = P(X ≤ √y) = (√y)³/8 = y^{3/2}/8 for 0 ≤ y ≤ 4.
2. g(y) = (3/16)y^{1/2}.

#### Common mistakes

- Forgetting the earlier pieces' area when building F.

### SD4 Mean and variance

*Refs: AQA SD4, SD5 · Edexcel FS2 2.3 · OCR 5.03c, 5.03d · MEI SR22, SR27. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

```latex
E(X) = \int x f(x)\,dx,\quad E(X^2) = \int x^2 f(x)\,dx,\quad \mathrm{Var}(X) = E(X^2) - [E(X)]^2
```

- E(g(X)) = ∫g(x)f(x) dx. E(aX + b) = aE(X) + b, Var(aX + b) = a²Var(X).

#### Worked example

For f(x) = (3/8)x² on [0, 2], find E(X), Var(X) and E(1/X).

1. E(X) = (3/8)∫₀² x³ dx = (3/8)(4) = 1.5.
2. E(X²) = (3/8)(32/5) = 2.4. Var(X) = 2.4 − 2.25 = 0.15.
3. E(1/X) = (3/8)∫₀² x dx = (3/8)(2) = 0.75.

#### Common mistakes

- Forgetting to multiply by x (or x²) before integrating.

### SD7 The rectangular (continuous uniform) distribution

*Refs: AQA SD7 · Edexcel FS2 2.4. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- X ~ U[a, b]: f(x) = 1/(b − a) for a ≤ x ≤ b.
- E(X) = (a + b)/2, Var(X) = (b − a)²/12, F(x) = (x − a)/(b − a).
- Use it for rounding errors, or a random point on an interval.

#### Worked example

A length is rounded to the nearest cm. Find the mean and standard deviation of the rounding error.

1. Error ~ U[−0.5, 0.5]. Mean 0, variance 1/12, s.d. 0.289 cm.

X ~ U[2, 8]. Find P(X > 6.5) and Var(X).

1. P = 1.5/6 = 0.25. Var = 36/12 = 3.

### SF1 The exponential distribution

*Refs: AQA SF1, SF2, SF3, SF4. Level: A-level only (AQA option).*

#### What you need to know

```latex
f(x) = \lambda e^{-\lambda x}\ (x \ge 0),\qquad F(x) = 1 - e^{-\lambda x},\qquad E(X) = \frac1\lambda,\qquad \mathrm{Var}(X) = \frac1{\lambda^2}
```

- If events occur as a Poisson process with rate λ, the time between events (and the time to the first event) is exponential with parameter λ.
- The exponential is memoryless: P(X > s + t | X > s) = P(X > t).

#### Explained

Proofs: E(X) = ∫₀^∞ λxe^{−λx} dx; integrating by parts gives [−xe^{−λx}]₀^∞ + ∫e^{−λx} dx = 1/λ. Similarly E(X²) = 2/λ², so Var = 1/λ². The link with Poisson: the time T to the first event exceeds t exactly when there are no events in [0, t], so P(T > t) = e^{−λt}.

#### Worked example

Buses arrive as a Poisson process with rate 0.5 per minute. Find the probability of waiting more than 3 minutes, and the median wait.

1. T ~ Exp(0.5). P(T > 3) = e^{−1.5} = 0.223.
2. 1 − e^{−0.5m} = ½, m = 2 ln 2 = 1.39 minutes.

#### Common mistakes

- Using 1/λ as the parameter in the pdf.

### Check questions

1. f(x) = k(4 − x²) for 0 ≤ x ≤ 2. Find k, E(X) and P(X < 1). [6 marks]
2. X has cdf F(x) = (x² − 1)/8 for 1 ≤ x ≤ 3. Find the pdf, the median and the interquartile range. [6 marks]
3. f(x) = ¼(4x − x³) for 0 ≤ x ≤ 2. Find the mode. [3 marks]
4. X ~ U[0, 10]. Find E(X²) and P(|X − 5| < 2). [3 marks]
5. (AQA) The lifetime of a component is exponential with mean 200 hours. Find the probability it lasts more than 300 hours, and, given it has lasted 100 hours, the probability it lasts a further 300 hours. [4 marks]
6. (OCR) X ~ U[0, 1]. Find the pdf of Y = √X. [3 marks]

### Mark scheme

1. k[4x − x³/3]₀² = 16k/3 = 1, k = 3/16 (2). E(X) = (3/16)∫(4x − x³) dx = (3/16)(8 − 4) = 3/4 (2). P(X < 1) = (3/16)(4 − ⅓) = 11/16 (2).
2. f(x) = x/4 on [1, 3] (1). (m² − 1)/8 = ½, m = √5 = 2.236 (2). q₁ = √3, q₃ = √7 (2). IQR = 0.914 (1).
3. f′ = ¼(4 − 3x²) = 0 (1), x = 2/√3 = 1.155 (1), a maximum since f″ < 0 (1).
4. E(X²) = 100/3 (or Var + mean² = 25/3 + 25) (2). P(3 < X < 7) = 0.4 (1).
5. λ = 1/200. P(T > 300) = e^{−1.5} = 0.223 (2). Memoryless: same, 0.223 (2).
6. G(y) = P(√X ≤ y) = P(X ≤ y²) = y² (2). g(y) = 2y for 0 ≤ y ≤ 1 (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/further-maths/36-linear-combinations-of-random-variables.md -->

## Linear combinations of random variables

This file covers the expectation and variance of sums and other linear combinations of random variables, the difference between X₁ + X₂ and 2X, and linear combinations of independent normal variables.

### Overview

Expectation and variance of single variables are in [30-discrete-random-variables.md](30-discrete-random-variables.md) and [35-continuous-random-variables.md](35-continuous-random-variables.md). The normal distribution is in [../maths/22-statistical-distributions.md](../maths/22-statistical-distributions.md). The sample mean as a linear combination leads to [37-sampling-estimation-and-central-limit-theorem.md](37-sampling-estimation-and-central-limit-theorem.md).

#### Board differences

- AQA SD8 (E and Var of X + Y for independent X, Y). Edexcel FS2 4.1 (linear combinations of normal variables). OCR 5.04a, 5.04b. MEI SR28, SR29.
- AQA lists only the general results for X + Y; Edexcel, OCR and MEI apply them to normal distributions.

### SD8 Expectation and variance of combinations

*Refs: AQA SD8 · Edexcel FS2 4.1 · OCR 5.04a · MEI SR29. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

```latex
E(aX + bY) = aE(X) + bE(Y)
```

```latex
\mathrm{Var}(aX \pm bY) = a^2\,\mathrm{Var}(X) + b^2\,\mathrm{Var}(Y)\quad(X, Y\text{ independent})
```

- The expectation rule holds always; the variance rule needs independence.
- Var(X − Y) = Var(X) + Var(Y): variances add even when you subtract.
- X₁ + X₂ + ... + Xₙ (n independent copies of X) has mean nμ and variance nσ². But nX (one observation multiplied by n) has variance n²σ².

#### Explained

Subtracting a random quantity adds uncertainty, which is why the variances add. The difference between X₁ + X₂ and 2X matters: two separate bags of flour vary less in total, relative to their mean, than one bag doubled.

#### Worked example

X and Y are independent with E(X) = 5, Var(X) = 2, E(Y) = 3, Var(Y) = 1. Find E(2X − 3Y) and Var(2X − 3Y).

1. E = 10 − 9 = 1.
2. Var = 4(2) + 9(1) = 17.

X has variance 4. Compare Var(X₁ + X₂ + X₃) and Var(3X).

1. Var(X₁ + X₂ + X₃) = 12. Var(3X) = 36.

#### Common mistakes

- Var(X − Y) = Var(X) − Var(Y).
- Writing 3X when the question means three independent observations.

### Combinations of independent normal variables

*Refs: Edexcel FS2 4.1 · OCR 5.04b · MEI SR28, SR29. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- Any linear combination of independent normal variables is normal, with mean and variance from the rules above.

#### Worked example

Bags of flour have masses N(1000, 15²) grams. Find the probability that four bags have total mass less than 4050 g.

1. T ~ N(4000, 4 × 225 = 900), s.d. 30.
2. P(T < 4050) = Φ(50/30) = Φ(1.667) = 0.952.

X ~ N(50, 9) and Y ~ N(60, 16) independently. Find P(X > Y).

1. X − Y ~ N(−10, 25).
2. P(X − Y > 0) = P(Z > 2) = 0.0228.

A tin A has mass N(120, 4) and a lid B has mass N(38, 1). Find the probability that a tin weighs more than three times a lid.

1. A − 3B ~ N(120 − 114, 4 + 9) = N(6, 13).
2. P(A − 3B > 0) = Φ(6/√13) = Φ(1.664) = 0.952.

#### Common mistakes

- Using 3²σ² for the total of three independent lids.

### Check questions

1. X ~ N(20, 4), Y ~ N(15, 9) independently. Find the distribution of 3X − 2Y and P(3X − 2Y > 35). [5 marks]
2. A lift has a safe load of 1200 kg. Adults' masses are N(75, 12²). Find the probability that 15 adults exceed the safe load. [4 marks]
3. Eggs have masses N(60, 4²) grams and empty boxes N(30, 2²) grams. A full box holds 6 eggs. Find the probability that a full box weighs less than 380 g. [4 marks]
4. Two independent spinners give X with E(X) = 2, Var(X) = 1.5, and Y with E(Y) = 3.5, Var(Y) = 2.9. Find E(X + 2Y) and Var(X − 2Y). [3 marks]

### Mark scheme

1. Mean 60 − 30 = 30 (1). Variance 9(4) + 4(9) = 72 (2). P(Z > 5/√72 = 0.589) = 0.278 (2).
2. Total ~ N(1125, 15 × 144 = 2160) (2). P(T > 1200) = P(Z > 75/46.48 = 1.614) = 0.0533 (2).
3. Total ~ N(390, 6 × 16 + 4 = 100) (2). P(T < 380) = Φ(−1) = 0.159 (2).
4. E(X + 2Y) = 9 (1). Var(X − 2Y) = 1.5 + 4(2.9) = 13.1 (2).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/further-maths/34-chi-squared-tests.md -->

## Chi-squared tests: goodness of fit and contingency tables

This file covers the χ² statistic, tests for association in contingency tables, goodness of fit tests for given and fitted distributions, degrees of freedom, combining classes when expected frequencies are small, Yates' correction, and interpreting where association comes from.

### Overview

Hypothesis testing language is in A-level Maths ([../maths/23-hypothesis-testing.md](../maths/23-hypothesis-testing.md)). The distributions being fitted are in [30-discrete-random-variables.md](30-discrete-random-variables.md), [31-poisson-geometric-and-negative-binomial-distributions.md](31-poisson-geometric-and-negative-binomial-distributions.md) and [35-continuous-random-variables.md](35-continuous-random-variables.md). Chi-squared tests for a variance are in [39-tests-for-variances-chi-squared-and-f.md](39-tests-for-variances-chi-squared-and-f.md).

#### Board differences

- AQA SE1 to SE5 (contingency tables only, including Yates' correction for 2 × 2 tables). AQA does not include goodness of fit.
- Edexcel FS1 6.1: goodness of fit (discrete uniform, binomial, Poisson, geometric and others) and contingency tables. Edexcel does not use Yates' correction.
- OCR 5.06a to 5.06d: contingency tables and goodness of fit; 5.06c (Stage 2) extends to continuous distributions and others.
- MEI SH1 to SH4: contingency tables and goodness of fit, interpreting contributions to the statistic.
- All boards combine classes so that expected frequencies are at least 5.

### SE2 The chi-squared statistic

*Refs: AQA SE2, SE3 · Edexcel FS1 6.1 · OCR 5.06a, 5.06d · MEI SH1, SH2. Level: AS & A (OCR, MEI).*

#### What you need to know

```latex
X^2 = \sum\frac{(O_i - E_i)^2}{E_i}
```

- Under H₀, X² approximately follows χ²_ν, where ν (degrees of freedom) = number of cells (after combining) − number of constraints.
- Reject H₀ if X² is greater than the critical value. The test is always one-tailed (upper tail).
- Each Eᵢ should be at least 5; combine neighbouring classes if not.

#### Explained

Large differences between observed and expected frequencies make X² big. The degrees of freedom count how many expected frequencies are free to vary: the total is always fixed (one constraint), and each parameter estimated from the data costs one more.

### SE1 Contingency tables

*Refs: AQA SE1, SE2, SE3, SE4, SE5 · Edexcel FS1 6.1 · OCR 5.06a · MEI SH1, SH2. Level: AS & A (OCR, MEI).*

#### What you need to know

- H₀: no association between the two factors (they are independent). H₁: there is association.
- Expected frequency = (row total × column total)/grand total.
- For an r × c table, ν = (r − 1)(c − 1).
- (AQA) For a 2 × 2 table use Yates' correction: Σ(|O − E| − 0.5)²/E.
- Identify sources of association by comparing the largest contributions (O − E)²/E and whether O > E or O < E.

#### Worked example

60 pupils in each of two year groups chose a favourite club. Test at 5% for association.

| | Art | Chess | Drama |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Year 10 | 30 | 20 | 10 |
| Year 11 | 20 | 25 | 15 |

1. H₀: no association between year and club choice.
2. Column totals 50, 45, 25; grand total 120. Expected values: 25, 22.5, 12.5 in each row.
3. In each row the (O − E)² values are 25, 6.25, 6.25, so X² = 2 × [25/25 + 6.25/22.5 + 6.25/12.5] = 2 × [1 + 0.278 + 0.5] = 3.556.
4. ν = (2 − 1)(3 − 1) = 2. Critical value 5.991.
5. 3.556 < 5.991: do not reject H₀. No evidence of association.
6. The largest contributions (Art) show Year 10 chose art more than expected.

(AQA) 2 × 2 table with Yates' correction:

| | Pass | Fail |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Revised | 25 | 15 |
| Did not | 10 | 20 |

1. Expected: 20, 20 / 15, 15. |O − E| = 5 each.
2. X² = 4.5²(1/20 + 1/20 + 1/15 + 1/15) = 20.25 × 0.2333 = 4.725.
3. ν = 1, critical value 3.841 at 5%. Reject H₀: evidence of association; those who revised passed more often than expected.

#### Common mistakes

- Using percentages instead of frequencies.
- Forgetting to combine cells with E < 5 (which also reduces ν).

### Goodness of fit

*Refs: Edexcel FS1 6.1 · OCR 5.06b, 5.06c, 5.06d · MEI SH3, SH4. Level: AS & A (OCR 5.06c A-level only).*

#### What you need to know

- H₀: the data come from the stated distribution.
- Expected frequency = total × probability under H₀.
- ν = (number of classes after combining) − 1 − (number of parameters estimated from the data).

#### Worked example

A die is rolled 120 times: 15, 22, 18, 25, 16, 24. Test at 5% whether it is fair.

1. H₀: uniform, each E = 20.
2. X² = (25 + 4 + 4 + 25 + 16 + 16)/20 = 4.5.
3. ν = 5, critical value 11.07. Not significant: no evidence the die is biased.

Over 100 time intervals, counts of 0, 1, 2, 3 or more events were 30, 38, 20, 12, with sample mean 1.18. Test at 5% whether a Poisson model fits.

1. H₀: Poisson. Estimate λ = 1.18.
2. Expected: 100P(0) = 30.73, 100P(1) = 36.26, 100P(2) = 21.39, 100P(≥3) = 11.62.
3. X² = 0.017 + 0.084 + 0.091 + 0.012 = 0.204.
4. ν = 4 − 1 − 1 = 2, critical value 5.991. Not significant: a Poisson model fits well.

A genetics model predicts ratios 1 : 2 : 1. Observed 10, 28, 22 out of 60. Test at 5%.

1. E: 15, 30, 15. X² = 25/15 + 4/30 + 49/15 = 5.067.
2. ν = 2, critical value 5.991. Not significant at 5%.

#### Common mistakes

- Not subtracting 1 for each estimated parameter.
- Leaving the last class as "3" instead of "3 or more", so probabilities do not add to 1.

### Check questions

1. In a survey, 80 people were asked which of three brands they prefer, by age group. (a) State the null hypothesis. (b) Explain how to find an expected frequency. (c) The X² value is 7.2 for a 2 × 3 table. Complete the test at 5%. [5 marks]
2. (AQA) A 2 × 2 table has observed frequencies 18, 12 / 22, 28. Test for association at 5% using Yates' correction. [7 marks]
3. A spinner has five equal sectors. 100 spins give 14, 25, 19, 26, 16. Test at 10% whether it is fair. [5 marks]
4. 200 families with 3 children are recorded by number of girls: 0, 1, 2, 3 girls in 30, 80, 70, 20 families. Fit a binomial model B(3, p) estimating p from the data, and test the fit at 5%. [8 marks]

### Mark scheme

1. (a) No association between age group and preferred brand (1). (b) Row total × column total ÷ grand total (1). (c) ν = 2, critical value 5.991 (1). 7.2 > 5.991, reject H₀ (1). Evidence of association between age and brand (1).
2. Totals: rows 30, 50; columns 40, 40; grand 80 (1). Expected 15, 15 / 25, 25 (1). |O − E| = 3, corrected 2.5 (1). X² = 6.25(2/15 + 2/25) = 1.333 (2). ν = 1, critical value 3.841 (1). Not significant: no evidence of association (1).
3. E = 20 each (1). X² = (36 + 25 + 1 + 36 + 16)/20 = 5.7 (2). ν = 4, critical value at 10% 7.779 (1). Not significant: no evidence the spinner is unfair (1).
4. Mean girls = (80 + 140 + 60)/200 = 1.4, p = 1.4/3 = 0.4667 (2). Expected: 200(0.5333)³ = 30.3, 200 × 3(0.4667)(0.5333)² = 79.6, 200 × 3(0.4667)²(0.5333) = 69.7, 200(0.4667)³ = 20.3 (2). X² ≈ 0.01 (2). ν = 4 − 1 − 1 = 2, critical value 5.991 (1). Very good fit: accept the binomial model (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/further-maths/38-confidence-intervals-and-tests-for-means.md -->

## Confidence intervals and tests for means (z and t)

This file covers confidence intervals for a population mean (variance known, large samples, and small samples using the t-distribution), interpreting intervals, tests for a mean using z and t, tests and intervals for the difference between two means, and the paired t-test.

### Overview

Sampling distributions and the central limit theorem are in [37-sampling-estimation-and-central-limit-theorem.md](37-sampling-estimation-and-central-limit-theorem.md). The test for a normal mean with known variance is in A-level Maths ([../maths/23-hypothesis-testing.md](../maths/23-hypothesis-testing.md)). Tests for variances are in [39-tests-for-variances-chi-squared-and-f.md](39-tests-for-variances-chi-squared-and-f.md).

#### Board differences

- AQA SG1 (one-sample t-test), SH1 to SH4 (z and t confidence intervals for one mean).
- Edexcel FS2 5.1 to 5.5 (z intervals and tests, difference of two means, large samples), 7.1 to 7.3 (t-test and interval for one mean, paired t-test, two-sample t-test with pooled variance).
- OCR 5.05c, 5.05d (normal tests and intervals for a mean, using the CLT for large samples). OCR uses non-parametric tests rather than t (file 41).
- MEI SI7 to SI13 (confidence intervals, including t and for paired samples), SH5, SH6 (tests for a mean using z and t, and paired samples).

### SH1 Confidence intervals for a mean

*Refs: AQA SH1, SH2, SH3, SH4 · Edexcel FS2 5.2, 5.3, 5.5, 7.1 · OCR 5.05d · MEI SI7, SI8, SI9, SI12, SI13. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- Known σ (normal population, or any population with large n):

```latex
\bar x \pm z\frac{\sigma}{\sqrt n}\quad(z = 1.645, 1.960, 2.576\text{ for }90\%, 95\%, 99\%)
```

- Large sample, σ unknown: use s in place of σ (CLT).
- Small sample from a normal population, σ unknown:

```latex
\bar x \pm t_{n-1}\frac{s}{\sqrt n}
```

- A 95% interval is a random interval that contains μ in 95% of repeated samples. Wider for higher confidence, narrower for larger n.
- A value of μ outside the interval would be rejected by a two-tailed test at the matching level.

#### Worked example

A sample of 25 from N(μ, 16) has mean 52.3. Find a 95% confidence interval for μ.

1. 52.3 ± 1.96 × 4/5 = 52.3 ± 1.568.
2. (50.73, 53.87).

A sample of 100 has mean 3.42 and s = 0.8. Find a 95% interval for μ.

1. Large sample: 3.42 ± 1.96 × 0.08 = (3.26, 3.58).

Eight lengths (cm): 12.1, 11.8, 12.4, 12.0, 11.6, 12.3, 11.9, 12.5. Assuming normality, find a 95% interval for μ.

1. x̄ = 12.075, s = 0.3105, t₇ = 2.365.
2. 12.075 ± 2.365 × 0.3105/√8 = 12.075 ± 0.260 = (11.82, 12.33).

#### Common mistakes

- Using z for a small sample with unknown σ.
- Saying "there is a 95% probability μ is in this interval".

### SG1 Tests for a mean

*Refs: AQA SG1 · Edexcel FS2 5.4, 7.1 · OCR 5.05c · MEI SH5. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- z-test (σ known, or large n): Z = (x̄ − μ₀)/(σ/√n).
- t-test (small sample, normal population, σ unknown): T = (x̄ − μ₀)/(s/√n) with n − 1 degrees of freedom.

#### Worked example

For the eight lengths above, test at 5% whether the mean is less than 12.3 cm.

1. H₀: μ = 12.3, H₁: μ < 12.3.
2. t = (12.075 − 12.3)/(0.3105/√8) = −2.049.
3. Critical value −t₇(5%) = −1.895. −2.049 < −1.895: reject H₀. Evidence the mean is less than 12.3 cm.

#### Common mistakes

- Using n instead of n − 1 for the degrees of freedom.

### Two means and paired data

*Refs: Edexcel FS2 5.4, 7.2, 7.3 · MEI SI10, SI11, SH6. Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- Difference of means, large samples or known variances:

```latex
Z = \frac{(\bar x_1 - \bar x_2) - (\mu_1 - \mu_2)}{\sqrt{\dfrac{\sigma_1^2}{n_1} + \dfrac{\sigma_2^2}{n_2}}}
```

- (Edexcel) Two small samples from normal populations with a common variance: pool the variance and use t with n₁ + n₂ − 2 degrees of freedom:

```latex
s_p^2 = \frac{(n_1-1)s_1^2 + (n_2-1)s_2^2}{n_1+n_2-2}
```

- Paired data (the same subjects measured twice): work with the differences d and do a one-sample test on them with n − 1 degrees of freedom.
- (MEI SI10) Pair the data when each value in one sample is naturally linked to one value in the other.

#### Worked example

Sample 1: n = 40, mean 68.2, s = 5. Sample 2: n = 50, mean 65.9, s = 6. Test at 5% for a difference in population means.

1. H₀: μ₁ = μ₂, H₁: μ₁ ≠ μ₂.
2. z = 2.3/√(25/40 + 36/50) = 1.983.
3. Critical values ±1.96. 1.983 > 1.96: reject H₀ (just). Evidence of a difference.

(Edexcel) Two normal samples: n₁ = 6, x̄₁ = 24.5, s₁² = 4.1; n₂ = 8, x̄₂ = 22.1, s₂² = 3.6. Test at 5% (two-tailed) for a difference, assuming equal variances.

1. s_p² = (5 × 4.1 + 7 × 3.6)/12 = 3.808.
2. t = 2.4/√(3.808(1/6 + 1/8)) = 2.277.
3. t₁₂(2.5%) = 2.179. Reject H₀: evidence the means differ.

Eight students sit a test before and after a course. Differences (after − before): 3, −1, 4, 2, 5, 0, 3, 2. Test at 5% whether scores improved.

1. d̄ = 2.25, s_d = 1.982.
2. t = 2.25/(1.982/√8) = 3.211. t₇(5%) = 1.895.
3. Reject H₀: evidence of improvement.

#### Common mistakes

- Treating paired data as two independent samples.

### Check questions

1. A sample of 16 from N(μ, 9) has mean 20.4. Find a 99% confidence interval for μ. [3 marks]
2. Ten observations from a normal population have x̄ = 7.3 and s = 1.2. Find a 90% confidence interval for μ. [4 marks]
3. A 95% interval for μ is (14.2, 17.8) from a sample of 25 with σ known. Find x̄ and σ. [3 marks]
4. A machine is set to fill 500 ml. A sample of 12 gives x̄ = 497.5 ml, s = 3.8 ml. Test at 5% whether the machine under-fills. State an assumption. [6 marks]
5. Six athletes' times (s) before and after training: before 12.4, 12.9, 13.1, 12.6, 12.8, 13.0; after 12.2, 12.8, 12.7, 12.6, 12.5, 12.9. Test at 5% whether training reduced times. [7 marks]

### Mark scheme

1. 20.4 ± 2.576 × 3/4 (2) = (18.47, 22.33) (1).
2. t₉(5%) = 1.833 (1). 7.3 ± 1.833 × 1.2/√10 (2) = (6.60, 8.00) (1).
3. x̄ = 16 (1). 1.96σ/5 = 1.8 (1), σ = 4.59 (1).
4. H₀: μ = 500, H₁: μ < 500 (1). t = −2.5/(3.8/√12) = −2.279 (2). t₁₁(5%) = −1.796 (1). Reject H₀: evidence of under-filling (1). Assume volumes are normally distributed (1).
5. Differences (before − after): 0.2, 0.1, 0.4, 0, 0.3, 0.1 (1). d̄ = 0.1833, s_d = 0.1472 (2). t = 0.1833/(0.1472/√6) = 3.05 (2). t₅(5%) = 2.015 (1). Reject H₀: evidence training reduced times (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/further-maths/51-graph-theory.md -->

## Graph theory

This file covers the language of graphs, degree and the handshaking lemma, simple graphs, trees, complete and bipartite graphs, complements, adjacency matrices, digraphs, Eulerian and Hamiltonian graphs (including Ore's theorem), isomorphism, planarity (Euler's formula, Kuratowski's theorem, the planarity algorithm, thickness) and colouring arguments.

### Overview

Option content. Weighted graphs (networks) and their algorithms are in [52-network-algorithms.md](52-network-algorithms.md).

#### Board differences

- AQA DA1 to DA7: language, Eulerian, semi-Eulerian and Hamiltonian graphs, Euler's formula, Kuratowski's theorem, complete and bipartite graphs, adjacency matrices, complements, trees and isomorphism.
- Edexcel D1 1.4: the planarity algorithm (for graphs with a Hamiltonian cycle). Other graph language appears in the D1 glossary.
- OCR 7.02a to 7.02r: the widest coverage, including colouring arguments (7.02f), Ore's theorem (7.02i), digraphs (7.02k) and thickness (7.02o).
- MEI AN1, AN2: graph language and modelling problems with graphs.

### DA1 The language of graphs

*Refs: AQA DA1, DA5, DA6 · OCR 7.02a, 7.02b, 7.02c, 7.02d, 7.02e, 7.02k, 7.02q · MEI AN1, AN2. Level: AS & A (OCR, MEI).*

#### What you need to know

- A graph has vertices (nodes) joined by edges (arcs). The degree (order) of a vertex is the number of edge ends at it; a loop counts 2.
- Handshaking lemma: the sum of degrees = 2 × number of edges, so the number of odd vertices is even.
- Walk: any sequence of edges; trail: no edge repeated; path: no vertex repeated; cycle: a closed path.
- Simple graph: no loops or multiple edges. Connected: a path between every pair of vertices. Subgraph; subdivision (edges split by new degree-2 vertices).
- Tree: connected with no cycles; a tree with n vertices has n − 1 edges. Spanning tree: a subgraph tree including every vertex.
- Kₙ: complete graph, every pair joined, n(n − 1)/2 edges. K_{m,n}: complete bipartite graph, mn edges.
- Complement of a simple graph G: same vertices, with an edge exactly where G has none.
- Adjacency matrix: entry (i, j) is the number of edges between i and j.
- Digraph: edges have directions; in-degree and out-degree.

#### Worked example

A simple graph has 6 vertices with degrees 1, 2, 2, 3, 3, x. Find the possible values of x.

1. The degree sum 11 + x must be even, so x is odd.
2. In a simple graph with 6 vertices, x ≤ 5. So x = 1, 3 or 5 (each can be confirmed by drawing a graph).

How many edges does the complement of K_{3,4} have?

1. K₇ has 21 edges; K_{3,4} has 12. Complement 9 edges (a K₃ and a K₄, disjoint).

#### Common mistakes

- Counting a loop as degree 1.

### DA2 Eulerian and Hamiltonian graphs

*Refs: AQA DA2 · OCR 7.02g, 7.02h, 7.02i. Level: AS & A (OCR 7.02h, 7.02i A-level only).*

#### What you need to know

- Eulerian: a connected graph with a closed trail using every edge exactly once; this happens if and only if every vertex has even degree.
- Semi-Eulerian: exactly two odd vertices; an open trail using every edge starts at one and ends at the other.
- Hamiltonian: has a cycle visiting every vertex exactly once. There is no simple test.
- Ore's theorem: a simple graph with n ≥ 3 vertices is Hamiltonian if deg(u) + deg(v) ≥ n for every pair of non-adjacent vertices u, v. (A sufficient condition, not necessary.)

#### Worked example

Which complete graphs Kₙ are Eulerian?

1. Every vertex has degree n − 1. Eulerian if n − 1 is even: n odd.

Show that K_{3,3} is Hamiltonian, and use Ore's theorem on it.

1. Cycle A1 B1 A2 B2 A3 B3 A1 visits all 6 vertices.
2. Non-adjacent pairs are in the same set, each with degree 3: 3 + 3 = 6 ≥ 6. Ore's theorem confirms it.

#### Common mistakes

- Thinking a Hamiltonian cycle must use every edge.

### DA7 Isomorphism

*Refs: AQA DA7 · OCR 7.02j. Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- Two graphs are isomorphic if there is a one-to-one matching of vertices that preserves adjacency.
- Isomorphic graphs have the same numbers of vertices and edges and the same degree sequence; these are necessary, not sufficient. Look also for cycles of a given length.

#### Worked example

Are the 6-cycle C₆ and two disjoint triangles isomorphic?

1. Both have 6 vertices, 6 edges and all degrees 2.
2. C₆ is connected; two triangles are not. Not isomorphic.

### DA3 Planarity

*Refs: AQA DA3, DA4 · Edexcel D1 1.4 · OCR 7.02f, 7.02l, 7.02m, 7.02n, 7.02o. Level: A-level only (OCR); option (AQA, Edexcel).*

#### What you need to know

- A planar graph can be drawn with no edges crossing. It divides the plane into regions (faces), including the outside region.
- Euler's formula for a connected planar graph: V + R = E + 2 (equivalently V − E + R = 2).
- For a simple connected planar graph with V ≥ 3: E ≤ 3V − 6 (each region has at least 3 edges). If there are no triangles: E ≤ 2V − 4.
- K₅ and K_{3,3} are not planar. Kuratowski's theorem: a graph is planar if and only if it has no subgraph that is a subdivision of K₅ or K_{3,3}.
- Planarity algorithm (Edexcel): draw a Hamiltonian cycle as a polygon, list the other edges, and place them inside or outside so that none cross (edges that would cross go on opposite sides). If this cannot be done, the graph is not planar.
- Thickness (OCR): the least number of planar graphs whose union is the graph. K₅ and K_{3,3} have thickness 2.
- (OCR 7.02f) Colouring arguments, for example showing a graph is bipartite (2-colourable) or not.

#### Worked example

Show that K₅ is not planar.

1. V = 5, E = 10. If planar and simple, E ≤ 3V − 6 = 9. But E = 10. Not planar.

Show that K_{3,3} is not planar.

1. V = 6, E = 9. Bipartite, so no triangles: E ≤ 2V − 4 = 8. But E = 9. Not planar.

A connected planar graph has 8 vertices and 12 edges. How many regions?

1. R = E + 2 − V = 6.

#### Common mistakes

- Using E ≤ 3V − 6 to prove a graph is planar (it only shows non-planarity).
- Forgetting the outside region.

### Check questions

1. A graph has degree sequence 2, 2, 3, 3, 4, 4. (a) How many edges? (b) Is it Eulerian, semi-Eulerian or neither? [3 marks]
2. Draw a simple graph with 5 vertices that is Eulerian but not Hamiltonian. [2 marks]
3. Find the number of edges of K₈, K_{4,5} and the complement of K_{4,5}. [3 marks]
4. Explain why a tree with 9 vertices has 8 edges and at least two vertices of degree 1. [3 marks]
5. A simple connected planar graph has 10 vertices and every region is a triangle (including the outside). Find the number of edges and regions. [3 marks]
6. Show that the graph obtained from K₆ by deleting 2 edges is not planar. [3 marks]

### Mark scheme

1. (a) Sum 18, 9 edges (1). (b) Two odd vertices (1): semi-Eulerian (1).
2. E.g. two triangles sharing one vertex ("bow tie"): all degrees even, but the shared vertex would be visited twice in any cycle through all vertices (2).
3. 28 (1). 20 (1). K₉ has 36 edges, so 16 (1).
4. A tree is connected with no cycles; adding vertices one at a time each adds one edge, giving n − 1 = 8 (2). Degree sum 16 over 9 vertices with each degree ≥ 1, and the longest path's end vertices have degree 1 (1).
5. 3R = 2E and V + R = E + 2 (1). E = 3V − 6 = 24 (1). R = 16 (1).
6. V = 6, E = 15 − 2 = 13 (1). 3V − 6 = 12 (1). 13 > 12, so not planar (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/further-maths/52-network-algorithms.md -->

## Network algorithms: spanning trees, shortest paths, route inspection and the travelling salesperson

This file covers networks (weighted graphs), minimum spanning trees by Kruskal's and Prim's algorithms (including the matrix form of Prim), shortest paths by Dijkstra's and Floyd's algorithms, the route inspection (Chinese postman) problem, and upper and lower bounds for the travelling salesperson problem.

### Overview

Option content. Graph language and Eulerian graphs are in [51-graph-theory.md](51-graph-theory.md). The example network used below has vertices A to F and edges AB 7, AC 4, AD 9, BC 5, BE 6, CD 3, CE 8, DE 7, DF 6, EF 5 (total weight 60).

#### Board differences

- AQA DB1 to DB5: minimum spanning trees, route inspection, TSP bounds, and evaluating network models. AQA does not name Dijkstra's algorithm.
- Edexcel D1 2.1, 2.2 (Kruskal, Prim including on a distance matrix, Dijkstra, Floyd), 3.1, 3.2 (route inspection with up to four odd vertices; classical and practical TSP, nearest neighbour, lower bounds, tour improvement).
- OCR 7.02p, 7.02r, 7.04a to 7.04f (Stage 2 adds nearest neighbour, lower bounds and route inspection).
- MEI AN3 to AN7 (Kruskal, Prim, Dijkstra; their complexities).

### DB2 Minimum spanning trees

*Refs: AQA DB1, DB2 · Edexcel D1 2.1 · OCR 7.02p, 7.04a · MEI AN3, AN4, AN5, AN7. Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- A minimum spanning tree (minimum connector) connects all vertices with the least total weight.
- Kruskal: list edges in increasing weight; add each edge unless it forms a cycle; stop at n − 1 edges.
- Prim: start at any vertex; repeatedly add the least-weight edge joining a vertex in the tree to one outside.
- Prim on a distance matrix: cross out the chosen vertex's row, label its column, and look down the labelled columns for the smallest undeleted entry.
- (MEI AN7) Kruskal's, Prim's and Dijkstra's algorithms are quadratic (O(n²)) in the number of vertices for matrix implementations.

#### Worked example

Find a minimum spanning tree of the example network using Kruskal's algorithm.

1. Order: CD 3, AC 4, BC 5, EF 5, BE 6, DF 6, AB 7, DE 7, CE 8, AD 9.
2. Add CD, AC, BC, EF, BE. (DF and later edges are not needed once 5 edges are chosen.)
3. Total weight 3 + 4 + 5 + 5 + 6 = 23.

Prim's algorithm from A gives the order AC, CD, CB, BE, EF, the same tree.

#### Common mistakes

- In Kruskal, adding an edge that makes a cycle.
- In Prim, choosing the overall smallest edge rather than one connected to the tree.

### Shortest paths

*Refs: Edexcel D1 2.2 · OCR 7.04b · MEI AN6. Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- Dijkstra: give the start vertex the permanent label 0. From the latest permanent vertex, update working values of its neighbours (keep the smallest). Make the smallest working value permanent. Repeat. Trace the route back using edges whose weight equals the difference in labels.
- Floyd (Edexcel): uses a distance table and a route table, updating every entry through each vertex in turn; it finds shortest paths between all pairs.

#### Worked example

Use Dijkstra's algorithm to find the shortest distances from A in the example network.

1. A 0. Working: B 7, C 4, D 9.
2. C 4 permanent. Update D: 4 + 3 = 7; E: 4 + 8 = 12; B stays 7.
3. B 7 and D 7 permanent (either order). From B: E min(12, 13) = 12. From D: F 13, E stays 12.
4. E 12, F 13 permanent.
5. Shortest A to F: 13, route A–C–D–F.

#### Common mistakes

- Making a vertex permanent before it has the smallest working value.

### DB3 Route inspection

*Refs: AQA DB3 · Edexcel D1 3.1 · OCR 7.04e. Level: A-level only (OCR); option (AQA, Edexcel).*

#### What you need to know

- To traverse every edge and return to the start: if all vertices are even, the length is the total weight.
- Otherwise pair up the odd vertices so that the total of the shortest paths between pairs is least, and repeat those paths.
- With 4 odd vertices there are 3 pairings. If the route may start and finish at different vertices, repeat only the shortest path between one pair of odd vertices and start and finish at the other two.

#### Worked example

Find the shortest closed route along every edge of the example network.

1. Degrees: A 3, B 3, C 4, D 4, E 4, F 2. Odd: A, B.
2. Shortest A to B is 7 (direct). Repeat AB.
3. Route length 60 + 7 = 67.

#### Common mistakes

- Repeating edges between odd vertices without finding the shortest path.

### DB4 The travelling salesperson problem

*Refs: AQA DB4, DB5 · Edexcel D1 3.2 · OCR 7.04c, 7.04d, 7.04f. Level: A-level only (OCR); option (AQA, Edexcel).*

#### What you need to know

- Classical problem: visit each vertex exactly once and return. Practical problem: visit each vertex at least once (replace the network by a complete table of shortest distances).
- Upper bound: the nearest neighbour algorithm gives a tour; the best upper bound is the smallest tour found. (Or double the MST and shortcut.)
- Lower bound: delete a vertex, find the MST of the rest, then add the two shortest edges from the deleted vertex. The best lower bound is the largest found.
- Optimal tour length lies between the best lower and upper bounds; if they are equal, the tour is optimal.

#### Worked example

Find upper and lower bounds for the practical TSP on the example network.

1. Nearest neighbour from A (using shortest distances): A–C 4, C–D 3, D–F 6, F–E 5, E–B 6, B–A 7. Total 31.
2. Lower bound deleting A: MST of B to F is CD 3, BC 5, EF 5, BE 6 = 19. Two shortest edges from A: 4 and 7. Lower bound 30.
3. 30 ≤ optimal ≤ 31. (Here the optimal tour is 31.)

#### Common mistakes

- Taking the smallest lower bound as the best one.

### Check questions

1. Use Prim's algorithm on this distance matrix, starting from P, and state the weight of the tree. P–Q 8, P–R 5, P–S 9, Q–R 4, Q–S 6, R–S 7. [4 marks]
2. In the example network, use Dijkstra to find the shortest route from B to F. [4 marks]
3. A network has odd vertices W, X, Y, Z with shortest distances WX 5, WY 8, WZ 6, XY 7, XZ 9, YZ 4 and total weight 120. Find the length of the shortest closed route inspection, and the shortest route if it may start and end at different vertices. [5 marks]
4. Explain why the nearest neighbour algorithm gives an upper bound and the deleted-vertex method a lower bound. [3 marks]

### Mark scheme

1. P–R 5, R–Q 4, Q–S 6 (3). Weight 15 (1).
2. B 0; C 5, A 7, E 6 (1). C permanent 5: D 8 (1). E permanent 6: F 11 (1). Shortest B–E–F 11 (1).
3. Pairings: WX + YZ = 9, WY + XZ = 17, WZ + XY = 13 (2). Closed route 120 + 9 = 129 (1). Open route: repeat only the shortest pair distance, 4 (YZ), starting and finishing at W and X: 124 (2).
4. Nearest neighbour gives an actual tour, so the optimum is no longer (1). The deleted-vertex method: any tour minus a vertex is a spanning tree of the rest (weight ≥ MST) plus two edges at the vertex (weight ≥ the two shortest) (2).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/further-maths/55-network-flows.md -->

## Network flows

This file covers flows in directed networks with capacities, cuts and their values, the maximum flow-minimum cut theorem, finding flow-augmenting paths with the labelling procedure, supersources and supersinks, vertices with restricted capacity, and arcs with lower as well as upper capacities.

### Overview

Option content. Graph language is in [51-graph-theory.md](51-graph-theory.md); network flow problems can also be formulated as linear programmes ([54-linear-programming-and-simplex.md](54-linear-programming-and-simplex.md)).

The example network: source S, sink T, arcs S→A 10, S→B 8, A→B 3, A→C 7, B→D 9, C→T 8, D→C 4, D→T 6 (capacities).

#### Board differences

- AQA DC1 to DC7, including arcs with upper and lower capacities (DC6) and nodes of restricted capacity (DC7).
- Edexcel D2 3.1 to 3.5: cuts, the labelling procedure (forward and backward arrows), max flow-min cut, multiple sources and sinks, restricted vertices, and finding optimal flows.
- MEI AN10 to AN13: flows, cuts, max flow-min cut, and exploring network algorithms with software.
- OCR H245 does not include network flows.

### DC1 Flows and cuts

*Refs: AQA DC1, DC2 · Edexcel D2 3.1 · MEI AN10, AN11. Level: AS & A (MEI); option (AQA, Edexcel).*

#### What you need to know

- A feasible flow does not exceed any arc's capacity (and is at least any lower capacity) and is conserved at every vertex other than the source and sink (flow in = flow out).
- A cut divides the vertices into a set containing S and a set containing T. Its value (capacity) is the sum of capacities of arcs going from the S side to the T side; arcs going the other way count 0 (or their lower capacity, if any, is subtracted).

#### Worked example

Find the values of three cuts in the example network.

1. {S} | rest: S→A + S→B = 18.
2. {S, A, B} | {C, D, T}: A→C + B→D = 16.
3. {S, A, B, C, D} | {T}: C→T + D→T = 14.

#### Common mistakes

- Adding the capacity of an arc that crosses the cut from the T side to the S side.

### DC3 Maximum flow-minimum cut

*Refs: AQA DC3, DC5 · Edexcel D2 3.2, 3.3 · MEI AN12. Level: AS & A (MEI); option.*

#### What you need to know

- Max flow-min cut theorem: the maximum flow equals the minimum cut value.
- To prove a flow is maximal, find a cut whose value equals it.
- Labelling procedure: on each arc, a forward label shows spare capacity and a backward label shows the flow that could be removed. Find a path from S to T with positive labels (a flow-augmenting path), increase the flow by the smallest label along it, and update the labels. Repeat until no path exists.

#### Worked example

Find the maximum flow in the example network.

1. Augment S–A–C–T by 7. Then S–B–D–T by 6. Then S–B–D–C–T by 1 (C→T then has no spare capacity).
2. Now C→T and D→T are both full, so no flow-augmenting path reaches T.
3. Total flow 7 + 6 + 1 = 14.
4. The cut {S, A, B, C, D} | {T} has value 14, so 14 is maximal.

#### Common mistakes

- Stopping without proving maximality with a cut.

### DC4 Supersources, supersinks and restricted vertices

*Refs: AQA DC4, DC7 · Edexcel D2 3.4 · MEI AN13. Level: option.*

#### What you need to know

- Several sources: add a supersource joined to each with capacity equal to that source's total output (or unlimited). Similarly a supersink.
- A vertex V with capacity c: replace it by V₁ → V₂ with an arc of capacity c; arcs into V go to V₁, arcs out leave V₂.

#### Worked example

In the example network, D can process at most 8 units. How does this change the maximum flow?

1. Split D into D₁ → D₂ with capacity 8.
2. The current flow through D is 7 (6 to T and 1 to C), which is under 8. The maximum stays 14.

### DC6 Lower capacities

*Refs: AQA DC6 · Edexcel D2 3.5. Level: option.*

#### What you need to know

- If an arc must carry at least l and at most u, a feasible flow must respect both. A cut's value is the sum of upper capacities of arcs from S to T side minus the sum of lower capacities of arcs from T side to S side.
- Start from a feasible flow that meets the lower capacities, then augment.

#### Worked example

In a network, a cut has forward arcs with upper capacities 10 and 7, and a backward arc with lower capacity 2. Find its value.

1. 10 + 7 − 2 = 15.

### Check questions

1. In the example network, find the value of the cut {S, A} | {B, C, D, T}. [2 marks]
2. A network has arcs S→P 6, S→Q 5, P→Q 2, P→T 4, Q→T 7. Find the maximum flow and a minimum cut. [5 marks]
3. Explain how to deal with a network that has two sinks. [2 marks]
4. Explain why the value of any cut is an upper bound for any flow. [2 marks]

### Mark scheme

1. S→B 8 + A→B 3 + A→C 7 = 18 (2).
2. Flow: S–P–T 4, S–Q–T 5, S–P–Q–T 2: total 11 (3). Cut {S, P, Q} | {T}: 4 + 7 = 11 (1), so 11 is maximal (1).
3. Add a supersink joined from each sink by an arc of capacity at least that sink's demand (or unlimited) (2).
4. All flow from S to T must cross the cut, and the flow across it cannot exceed the capacities of the forward arcs (2).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/further-maths/54-linear-programming-and-simplex.md -->

## Linear programming and the simplex algorithm

This file covers formulating linear programming (LP) problems, graphical solution (objective line and vertex methods), integer solutions, slack variables, the simplex algorithm and interpreting tableaux, problems with ≥ constraints (two-stage simplex and big-M), and sensitivity of the solution to changes.

### Overview

Option content. Inequalities and regions are in A-level Maths ([../maths/02-algebra-indices-surds-quadratics-and-inequalities.md](../maths/02-algebra-indices-surds-quadratics-and-inequalities.md)). Game theory problems solved as LPs are in [56-game-theory.md](56-game-theory.md); transportation and allocation formulations in [57-transportation-and-allocation.md](57-transportation-and-allocation.md); network flow formulations in [55-network-flows.md](55-network-flows.md).

#### Board differences

- AQA DD1 to DD4: formulation, graphical solution, simplex for maximising and minimising, interpreting tableaux.
- Edexcel D1 5.1 to 5.4: formulation, graphical solution (including integer solutions), simplex for maximising, two-stage simplex and big-M for ≥ constraints.
- OCR 7.06a to 7.06f, 7.07a to 7.07f: formulation, graphical solution and sensitivity (7.06e), simplex and its geometric meaning.
- MEI AL1 to AL18: formulation (including network problems, AL6), integer LP, 3D visualisation, simplex, two-stage simplex, free variables (AL16) and interpreting software output (AL18).

### DD1 Formulating linear programmes

*Refs: AQA DD1 · Edexcel D1 5.1 · OCR 7.06a · MEI AL1, AL2, AL3, AL5, AL6. Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- Define the decision variables (with units), write the objective function to maximise or minimise, and list the constraints, including non-negativity.
- Recognise when variables must be integers.

#### Worked example

A bakery makes x trays of bread (profit £4, 2 hours of oven time, 4 kg flour) and y trays of cakes (profit £5, 3 hours, 1 kg flour). It has 12 oven hours and 14 kg of flour a day. Formulate the LP.

1. Maximise P = 4x + 5y.
2. Subject to 2x + 3y ≤ 12, 4x + y ≤ 14, x ≥ 0, y ≥ 0.

#### Common mistakes

- Leaving out non-negativity, or not defining variables.

### DD2 Graphical solution

*Refs: AQA DD2 · Edexcel D1 5.2 · OCR 7.06c, 7.06d, 7.06e, 7.06f · MEI AL7, AL8, AL9, AL10, AL11. Level: AS & A.*

#### What you need to know

- Draw each constraint line and shade the unwanted side; the feasible region is left clear.
- The optimum is at a vertex: slide the objective line (ruler method) or evaluate the objective at each vertex.
- For integer solutions, check integer points near the optimal vertex.
- Sensitivity: changing a coefficient in the objective changes the gradient of the objective line and may move the optimum to a different vertex.

#### Worked example
